funding:
G.O. was supported by the Serrapilheira Institute (grant number Serra – 2211-42049), FAPERJ (grants E-26/201.397/2021 and E-26/204.532/2024) and CNPq (grants 303166/2022-3). J.C. and E.L. were supported by ANR-19-CE40-0024 (CHAllenges in MAthematical NEuroscience).

A
Proof of Theorem
3.3

The stationarity of the process
X
follows from the fact that its construction is invariant under time shift. Hence, to conclude the proof, it remains to show that, conditionally on each realization of the random environment
\theta
,
X
is Markovian and has the transition probabilities given by (
2
) and (
1
).

The Markovianity of the process and the conditional independence of its coordinates follow from its representation as a function of an i.i.d. sequence. The compatibility of the transition probabilities follows from simple computations.

Markovianity of the process

Observe that (
29
) implies that for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, there exists a deterministic function
f_{\theta,i}:\{0,1\}^{N}\times\{0,1\}\times\{0,1,\ldots,N\}\to\{0,1\}
such that for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
,

X_{i,t}=f_{\theta,i}(X_{t-1},\xi_{i,t},J_{i,t}).

Then defining the function

f_{\theta}(x,\xi,J)=(f_{\theta,1}(x,\xi_{1},J_{1}),\ldots,f_{\theta,N}(x,\xi_{N},J_{N})),

where
x\in\{0,1\}^{N}
,
\xi=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{N})\in\{0,1\}^{N}
and
J=(J_{1},\ldots,J_{N})\in\{0,1,\ldots,N\}^{N}
, we can write for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
,

X_{t}=f_{\theta}(X_{t-1},\xi_{t},J_{t}),

where
\xi_{t}=(\xi_{1,t},\ldots,\xi_{N,t})
and
J_{t}=(J_{1,t},\ldots,J_{N,t})
. This representation together with the fact the sequence
(\xi_{t},J_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is i.i.d ensures that
(X_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a Markov chain. Moreover, since the pairs
(\xi_{1,t},J_{1,t}),\ldots,(\xi_{N,t},J_{N,t})
are independent, the random variables
X_{i,t}=f_{\theta,i}(X_{t-1},\xi_{i,t},J_{i,t})
,
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, are clearly conditionally independent given
X_{t-1}
.

Transition probability

It remains to show that
I\!\!P_{\theta}(X_{i,t}=1|X_{t-1}=x)=p_{\theta,i}(x)
for all
x\in\{0,1\}^{N}
, where
p_{\theta,i}(x)
is given by (
1
).
To see that, write
z=(i,t)
and observe that
(
29
) allows us to write,

I\!\!P_{\theta}(X_{z}=1|X_{t-1}=x)=I\!\!P_{\theta}(J_{z}=0,\xi_{z}=1)+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{P}_{+}}\theta_{ij}x_{j}I\!\!P_{\theta}(J_{z}=j|X_{t-1}=x)\\
+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{P}_{-}}\theta_{ij}(1-x_{j})I\!\!P_{\theta}(J_{z}=j|X_{t-1}=x).

Using that
X_{t-1}
is measurable with respect to
\sigma(\xi_{k,s},J_{k,s},s\leq t-1,k\in\{1,\ldots,N\})
and the fact that both

J_{z}
and
\xi_{z}
are independent of all random variables
J_{k,s}
,
\xi_{k,s}
for
s\leq t-1
and
k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, we have that

I\!\!P_{\theta}(J_{z}=0,\xi_{z}=1|X_{t-1}=x)=I\!\!P_{\theta}(J_{z}=0,\xi_{z}=1),

and also that for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,N\},

I\!\!P_{\theta}(J_{z}=j|X_{t-1}=x)=I\!\!P_{\theta}(J_{z}=j)=\frac{(1-\lambda)}{N}.

As a consequence, it follows that

I\!\!P_{\theta}(X=1|X_{t-1}=x)=I\!\!P_{\theta}(J_{z}=0,\xi_{z}=1)+\frac{(1-\lambda)}{N}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{P}_{+}}\theta_{ij}x_{j}+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{P}_{-}}\theta_{ij}(1-x_{j}).

Since the random variables
J_{z}
and
\xi_{z}
are also independent, and
I\!\!P_{\theta}(J_{z}=0)=\lambda,

I\!\!P_{\theta}(\xi_{z}=1)=\beta
and
\mu=\lambda\beta
, it follows that
I\!\!P_{\theta}(X_{i,t}=1|X_{t-1}=x)=p_{\theta,i}(x)
, concluding the proof.

B
Coalescence couplings

This section is devoted to the proof of two lemmas which relate independence and coalescence. It is structured as follows. First, the two lemmas are stated. Next, the construction of the coupling is detailed. After that, some properties of the coupling are provided. Finally, the proofs of the two lemmas are given.

B.1
Two main lemmas

Here are two lemmas that relate coalescence with independence via couplings. The tilde and hat versions of
X
defined below respect some additional spatial independence properties. The objective of the two lemmas is to prove that these versions can be coupled in such a way that they are equal except on an event related with coalescence (which in turn we expect to be of small probability).

Lemma B.1
(All independent coupling).

Let
k\geq 2
and consider
k
different points
z_{1},\dots,z_{k}
in
\mathcal{Z}
. There exist random variables
\tilde{X}_{z_{1}},\dots,\tilde{X}_{z_{k}}
satisfying the following properties: for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,

(i)

\tilde{X}_{z_{i}}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{d}}}\,X_{z_{i}}
(i.e.,
\tilde{X}_{z_{i}}
and
X_{z_{i}}
have the same distribution);

(ii)

\tilde{X}_{z_{i}}
is independent of
(X_{z_{j}},\tilde{X}_{z_{j}})_{j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\setminus\{i\}};

(iii)

\{\tilde{X}_{z_{i}}\neq X_{z_{i}}\}\subset\cup_{j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\setminus\{i\}}\{z_{i}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{j}\}.

Lemma B.2
(Blockwise independence).

Let
z_{1},\dots,z_{4}
be four different points in
\mathcal{Z}
. There exist random variables
\tilde{X}_{z_{1}},\dots,\tilde{X}_{z_{4}}
and
\hat{X}_{z_{1}},\hat{X}_{z_{2}}
satisfying the following properties:

(i)

(\hat{X}_{z_{1}},\hat{X}_{z_{2}})\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{d}}}\,(X_{z_{1}},X_{z_{2}})
;

(ii)

(\hat{X}_{z_{1}},\hat{X}_{z_{2}})
is independent of
(X_{z_{3}},X_{z_{4}},\tilde{X}_{z_{3}},\tilde{X}_{z_{4}})
;

(iii)

\{\hat{X}_{z_{1}}\neq X_{z_{1}}\}\subset\cup_{k=3}^{4}\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{k}\}\cup\left(\{\tau^{R}_{z_{1}}<t_{3}\vee t_{4}\}\cap(\cup_{k=3}^{4}\{z_{2}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{k}\})\right)
,

(iv)

\{\hat{X}_{z_{2}}\neq X_{z_{2}}\}\subset\cup_{k=3}^{4}\{z_{2}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{k}\}\cup\left(\{\tau^{R}_{z_{2}}<t_{3}\vee t_{4}\}\cap(\cup_{k=3}^{4}\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{k}\})\right)
.

(v)

\hat{X}_{z_{1}}
is independent of
\tilde{X}_{z_{2}}
and
\hat{X}_{z_{2}}
is independent of
\tilde{X}_{z_{1}}
.

Moreover, for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,4\}
,

(iv)

\tilde{X}_{z_{i}}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{d}}}\,X_{z_{i}}
;

(v)

\tilde{X}_{z_{i}}
is independent of
(\tilde{X}_{z_{j}},X_{z_{j}},I^{z_{j}})_{j\in\{1,\ldots,4\}\setminus\{i\}}
;

(vi)

\{\tilde{X}_{z_{i}}\neq X_{z_{i}}\}\subset\cup_{j\in\{1,\ldots,4\}\setminus\{i\}}\{z_{i}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{j}\}.

Remark B.3
.

Let us describe with some words Item (iii) above: if the hat version
\hat{X}_{z_{1}}
is different from the standard version
X_{z_{1}}
, then we know that

•

z_{1}
coalesces with
z_{3}
or
z_{4}
, or

•

z_{2}
coalesces with
z_{3}
or
z_{4}
and the (backward) regeneration time of
z_{1}
is less than the starting times of
z_{3}
or
z_{4}
.

B.2
Construction of the couplings

Let us denote
(J,\xi)
the sequence
(J_{z},\xi_{z})_{z}
defined in the backward regeneration representation of the process
X
. Since we are working for a fixed matrix
\theta
in this section, we omit its dependence in what follows. Due to the backward regeneration representation there exist measurable functions
f
,
g
,
r
and
\tau
(which depend on
\theta
) such that, for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
,

I^{z}=(I^{z}_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{Z}}=f(z,J)\text{ and }X_{z}=g(I^{z},\xi_{r(I^{z})}),

where
r(I^{z})=(I^{z}_{\tau(I^{z})},\tau(I^{z}))
is the regenerating site associated with
z
and
\tau(I^{z})=\inf\{s\in\mathbb{Z}:I^{z}_{s}>0\}
is the last time that the random walk
I^{z}
is not in the state
0
. In what follows, we denote
I=(I^{z})_{z\in\cal Z}
.

Now, consider
5
independent sequences

(J^{(1)},\xi^{(1)}),\ldots,(J^{(5)},\xi^{(5)}),

all distributed as the sequence
(J,\xi)
, and which are furthermore independent of
(J,\xi)
. By convenience of notation, let us denote
(J^{(0)},\xi^{(0)}):=(J,\xi)
. In other words, the layer (0) corresponds to the original process, and we add five i.i.d. layers on top of that. We then define the backward random walks
I^{(i)}=(I^{(i),z})_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}
by
I^{(i),z}=f(z,J^{(i)})
, for each
0\leq i\leq 5
. Clearly, the collections of random walks
I^{(0)},\ldots,I^{(5)}
are independent. We will need the layers
(0)-(4)
to construct the tilde versions, and the layers
(0)
and
(5)
to construct the hat versions.

Construction of the tilde versions

For
i\in\{1,\ldots,4\}
, let us denote

{\tau}^{c}_{i}=\sup\left\{\tau^{c}_{z_{i},z_{j}}:j\in\{1,\ldots,4\}\setminus\{i\}\right\},

the first time at which
I^{z_{i}}
coalesces with (at least) one of the
3
other random walks (observe that
{\tau}^{c}_{i}=-\infty
if
I^{z_{i}}
does not coalesce with any of the other
3
random walks), and denote
z^{c}_{i}=(I^{z_{i}}_{{\tau}^{c}_{i}},{\tau}^{c}_{i})
the site of coalescence in case
{\tau}^{c}_{i}>-\infty
.
Then, for all
1\leq i\leq 4
and
s\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define

\tilde{I}^{z_{i}}_{s}=I^{(0),z_{i}}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{s>\tau^{c}_{i}}+I^{(i),z^{c}_{i}}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{s\leq\tau^{c}_{i}}.

(39)

In words, the random walk
\tilde{I}^{z_{i}}
follows the random walk
I^{z_{i}}
until it coalesces with one of the random walks
I^{z_{j}}
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,4\}\setminus\{i\}
. After this time, the random walk
\tilde{I}^{z_{i}}
follows the independent random walk
I^{(i),z_{i}^{c}}
associated with the site of coalescence
z_{i}^{c}
. With the notation
\tilde{L}^{z_{i}}_{s}=i\cdot 1_{s\leq{\tau}^{c}_{i}}\in\{0,\dots,4\}
, Equation (
39
) rewrites as

\tilde{I}^{z_{i}}_{s}=I^{(0),z_{i}}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\tilde{L}^{z_{i}}_{s}=0}+I^{(i),z^{c}_{i}}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\tilde{L}^{z_{i}}_{s}=i},

and some computations give the following easy recursion formula:

\tilde{I}^{z_{i}}_{s-1}=J_{(\tilde{I}^{z_{i}}_{s},s)}^{(\tilde{L}^{z_{i}}_{s})},\text{ for all $s\leq t_{i}$},

(40)

which can be compared with Equation (
28
).
Moreover, remark that the layer processes
\tilde{L}
are measurable functions of the state processes
\tilde{I}
since

\tau^{c}_{z_{i},z_{j}}=\sup\left\{s\in\mathbb{Z}:I^{z_{i}}_{s}=I^{z_{j}}_{s}\notin\{0,\infty\}\right\}=\sup\left\{s\in\mathbb{Z}:\tilde{I}^{z_{i}}_{s}=\tilde{I}^{z_{j}}_{s}\notin\{0,\infty\}\right\}.

Construction of the hat versions

Let us denote, for
i=1,2
,

\hat{\tau}_{i}=\sup\left\{\tau^{c}_{z_{i},z_{j}}:j\in\{3,4\}\right\},

and denote
\hat{z}^{c}_{i}=(I^{z_{i}}_{\hat{\tau}_{i}},\hat{\tau}_{i})
the corresponding site of coalescence in case
\hat{\tau}_{i}>-\infty
. For instance,
\hat{\tau}_{1}
corresponds to the first time at which
I^{z_{1}}
coalesces with either
I^{z_{3}}
or
I^{z_{4}}
.
Then, for
i=1,2
, we define the auxiliary process, for all
s\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\hat{I}^{(5),z_{i}}_{s}=I^{z_{i}}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{s>\hat{\tau}_{i}}+I^{(5),\hat{z}^{c}_{i}}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{s\leq\hat{\tau}_{i}}.

The construction is not yet done since these two random walks may be missing some coalescence in order to mimic the joint distribution of
(X_{z_{1}},X_{z_{2}}).

To circumvent this problem, we define

\hat{\tau}_{1,2}=\sup\left\{s\in\mathbb{Z}:\hat{I}^{(5),z_{1}}_{s}=\hat{I}^{(5),z_{2}}_{s}\notin\{0,\infty\}\right\},

and

\hat{I}^{z_{i}}=\hat{I}^{(5),z_{i}},\mbox{ if }\hat{\tau}_{1,2}\leq\hat{\tau}_{i},

while we put, for all
s\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\hat{I}^{z_{i}}_{s}=I^{z_{i}}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{s>\hat{\tau}_{1,2}}+\hat{I}^{(5),z_{3-i}}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{s\leq\hat{\tau}_{1,2}},\mbox{ if }\hat{\tau}_{1,2}>\hat{\tau}_{i}.

(41)

For instance, if
I^{z_{1}}
first coalesces, say, with
I^{z_{3}},
then it switches to its hat-version
\hat{I}^{z_{1}}
and remains stuck to this version forever. However, if
I^{z_{2}}
hits
\hat{I}^{z_{1}}
before hitting
I^{z_{3}}
or
I^{z_{4}},
then it coalesces with
\hat{I}^{z_{1}}
and remains stuck to it forever.
In words, we do not modify the random walk with the first (the largest in time) coalescence time
\hat{\tau}_{i}
, but we possibly modify the other one in between the times
\hat{\tau}_{1}
and
\hat{\tau}_{2}
. Like Equation (
40
), we have the following recursion:

\hat{I}^{z_{i}}_{s-1}=J_{(\hat{I}^{z_{i}}_{s},s)}^{(\hat{L}^{z_{i}}_{s})},\text{ for all $s\leq t_{i}$},

(42)

where

\hat{L}^{z_{1}}_{s}=5\cdot 1_{s\leq\hat{\tau}_{1}}+5\cdot 1_{\hat{\tau}_{1}<s\leq\hat{\tau}_{1,2}}1_{\hat{\tau}_{1,2}\leq\hat{\tau}_{2}}

(43)

with a similar definition for
\hat{L}^{z_{2}}_{s}.
In other words, in any case, process
1
is in layer
5
starting from time
\hat{\tau}_{1}
, that is
\hat{L}^{z_{1}}_{s}=5
for
s\leq\hat{\tau}_{1}
. But, if process
1
coalesces with process
2
(at time
\hat{\tau}_{1,2}
) and if process
2
is already evolving on layer
5
at that time, then process
1
switches to layer
5
at that coalescence time
\hat{\tau}_{1,2}
.

Once again, the layer processes
\hat{L}^{z_{1}}
and
\hat{L}^{z_{2}}
are measurable functions of the state processes
(\hat{I}^{z_{1}},\hat{I}^{z_{2}},I^{z_{3}},I^{z_{4}})
.

B.3
Properties of the couplings

First, let us formalize the fact that the processes
I
do not depend on the whole sequences
J^{(0)},\dots,J^{(5)}
but merely on a small subset of those. Let
\gamma\in\{0,1,\dots,N,\infty\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\ell\in\{0,\dots,5\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be some generic trajectories of the processes
I
and
L
.
Let us then define
J_{\gamma}^{\ell}=(J_{(\gamma_{s},s)}^{(\ell_{s})})_{s\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\{0,1,\dots,N\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
and, for all
z=(i,t)\in\mathcal{Z}
,
f^{|}_{z}:\{0,1,\dots,N\}^{\mathbb{Z}}\to\{0,1,\dots,N,\infty\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
given by

(f^{|}_{z}(J_{\gamma}^{\ell}))_{s}=\begin{cases}\infty&\text{if $s>t$}\\
i&\text{if $s=t$}\\
J_{(\gamma_{s+1},s+1)}^{(\ell_{s+1})}&\text{else}.\end{cases}

Finally, let us remark that:

1.

the definition of
I^{z_{i}}
implies that
\{I^{z_{i}}=\gamma\}=\{f^{|}_{z_{i}}(J_{\gamma}^{\ell^{\emptyset}})=\gamma\}
where
\ell^{\emptyset}_{s}=0
for all
s\in\mathbb{Z}
;

2.

Equation (
40
) and the measurability of the layer processes imply that for all
\tilde{\gamma}^{1},\dots,\tilde{\gamma}^{4}\in\{0,1,\dots,N,\infty\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, there exist
\tilde{\ell}^{1},\dots,\tilde{\ell}^{4}\in\{0,\dots,4\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
such that

\cap_{i=1}^{4}\{\tilde{I}^{z_{i}}=\tilde{\gamma}^{i}\}=\cap_{i=1}^{4}\{\tilde{I}^{z_{i}}=\tilde{\gamma}^{i},\tilde{L}^{z_{i}}=\tilde{\ell}^{i}\}=\cap_{i=1}^{4}\{f^{|}_{z_{i}}(J_{\tilde{\gamma}^{i}}^{\tilde{\ell}^{i}})=\tilde{\gamma}^{i}\};

3.

Equation (
42
) and the measurability of the layer processes imply that for all choices
\hat{\gamma}^{1},\hat{\gamma}^{2},\gamma^{3},\gamma^{4},\tilde{\gamma}^{3},\tilde{\gamma}^{4}\in\{0,1,\dots,N,\infty\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, there exist
\hat{\ell}^{1},\hat{\ell}^{2}\in\{0,5\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\tilde{\ell}^{3},\tilde{\ell}^{4}\in\{0,3,4\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
such that

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left(\cap_{i=1}^{2}\{\hat{I}^{z_{i}}=\hat{\gamma}^{i}\}\right)\cap\left(\cap_{j=3}^{4}\{I^{z_{j}}=\gamma^{j},\tilde{I}^{z_{j}}=\tilde{\gamma}^{j}\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\cap_{i=1}^{2}\{\hat{I}^{z_{i}}=\hat{\gamma}^{i},\hat{L}^{z_{i}}=\hat{\ell}^{i}\}\right)\cap\left(\cap_{j=3}^{4}\{I^{z_{j}}=\gamma^{j},\tilde{I}^{z_{j}}=\tilde{\gamma}^{j},\tilde{L}^{z_{j}}=\tilde{\ell}^{j}\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\cap_{i=1}^{2}\{f^{|}_{z_{i}}(J_{\hat{\gamma}^{i}}^{\hat{\ell}^{i}})=\hat{\gamma}^{i}\}\right)\cap\left(\cap_{j=3}^{4}\{f^{|}_{z_{j}}(J_{\gamma^{j}}^{\ell^{\emptyset}})=\gamma^{j},f^{|}_{z_{j}}(J_{\tilde{\gamma}^{j}}^{\tilde{\ell}^{j}})=\tilde{\gamma}^{j}\}\right).

First, let us summarize the coupling properties of the processes
I
.

Proposition B.4
.

The processes
I
,
\tilde{I}
and
\hat{I}
satisfy the following coupling properties:

(i)

for all
i=1,\dots,4
,
\{\tilde{I}^{z_{i}}\neq I^{z_{i}}\}=\cup_{j\in\{1,\ldots,4\}\setminus\{i\}}\{z_{i}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{j}\}
,

(ii)

we have that

\{\hat{I}^{z_{1}}\neq I^{z_{1}}\}\subset\bigcup_{k=3}^{4}\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{k}\}\cup\left(\bigcup_{k=3}^{4}\{z_{2}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{k}\}\cap\{\tau^{R}_{z_{1}}<t_{3}\vee t_{4}\}\right)

and

\{\hat{I}^{z_{2}}\neq I^{z_{2}}\}\subset\bigcup_{k=3}^{4}\{z_{2}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{k}\}\cup\left(\bigcup_{k=3}^{4}\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{k}\}\cap\{\tau^{R}_{z_{2}}<t_{3}\vee t_{4}\}\right).

This follows directly from the construction of the processes and the remark that

\left\{\hat{\tau}_{i}<\hat{\tau}_{1,2}\leq\hat{\tau}_{3-i}\right\}\subset\bigcup_{k=3}^{4}\{z_{3-i}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{k}\}\cap\{\tau^{R}_{z_{i}}<t_{3}\vee t_{4}\}.

∎

We are now in position to prove the following independence properties of the processes
I
.

Proposition B.5
.

The processes
I
,
\tilde{I}
and
\hat{I}
satisfy the following independence properties:

(i)

for all
i
,
\tilde{I}^{z_{i}}
is independent of
(I^{z_{j}},\tilde{I}^{z_{j}})_{j\neq i}
;

(ii)

(\hat{I}^{z_{1}},\hat{I}^{z_{2}})
is independent of
(I^{z_{j}},\tilde{I}^{z_{j}})_{j=3,4}
.

(iii)

\hat{I}^{z_{1}}
is independent of
\tilde{I}^{z_{2}},
and
\hat{I}^{z_{2}}
is independent of
\tilde{I}^{z_{1}}.

Proof of (i)

Assume without loss of generality that
i=1
. Let
\tilde{\gamma}^{1},\dots,\tilde{\gamma}^{4},\gamma^{2},\dots,\gamma^{4}\in\{0,1,\dots,2N,\infty\}^{\mathbb{Z}},
and let
\tilde{\ell}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\ell}_{4}
be the associated layer processes, which are uniquely defined being measurable functions of
\tilde{\gamma}^{1},\dots,\tilde{\gamma}^{4},\gamma^{2},\dots,\gamma^{4}.
By the point 2 above, we have

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\cap_{i=1,\dots,4}\{\tilde{I}^{z_{1}}=\tilde{\gamma}^{1}\}\right)\cap\left(\cap_{j=2,\dots,4}\{I^{z_{j}}=\gamma^{j}\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{f^{|}_{z_{1}}(J_{\tilde{\gamma}^{1}}^{\tilde{\ell}^{1}})=\tilde{\gamma}^{1}\}\cap\left(\cap_{j=2}^{4}\{f^{|}_{z_{j}}(J_{\tilde{\gamma}^{j}}^{\tilde{\ell}^{j}})=\tilde{\gamma}^{j},f^{|}_{z_{j}}(J_{\gamma^{j}}^{\ell^{\emptyset}})=\gamma^{j}\}\right)

By construction, if
I\!\!P(A)
is non null, then the set
\{(\tilde{\gamma}^{1}_{s},\tilde{\ell}^{1}_{s},s),s\in\mathbb{Z},\tilde{\gamma}^{1}_{s}\notin\{0,\infty\}\}
is disjoint from the sets
\{(\tilde{\gamma}^{j}_{s},\tilde{\ell}^{j}_{s},s),s\in\mathbb{Z},\tilde{\gamma}^{j}_{s}\notin\{0,\infty\}\}
and
\{(\gamma^{j}_{s},0,s),s\in\mathbb{Z},\gamma^{j}_{s}\notin\{0,\infty\}\}
for
j>1
so that the two events in the final expression of
A
are independent, and Item (i) follows.

Proof of (ii)

Let
\hat{\gamma}^{1},\hat{\gamma}^{2},\gamma^{3},\gamma^{4},\tilde{\gamma}^{3},\tilde{\gamma}^{4}\in\{0,1,\dots,N,\infty\}^{\mathbb{Z}},
and let
\hat{l}^{1},\hat{l}^{2},\tilde{l}^{3},\tilde{l}^{4}
be the associated layer processes. By the point 3 above, we have

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\cap_{i=1}^{2}\{\hat{I}^{z_{i}}=\hat{\gamma}^{i}\}\right)\cap\left(\cap_{j=3}^{4}\{I^{z_{j}}=\gamma^{j},\tilde{I}^{z_{j}}=\tilde{\gamma}^{j}\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\cap_{i=1}^{2}\{f^{|}_{z_{i}}(J_{\hat{\gamma}^{i}}^{\hat{\ell}^{i}})=\hat{\gamma}^{i}\}\right)\cap\left(\cap_{j=3}^{4}\{f^{|}_{z_{j}}(J_{\gamma^{j}}^{\ell^{\emptyset}})=\gamma^{j},f^{|}_{z_{j}}(J_{\tilde{\gamma}^{j}}^{\tilde{\ell}^{j}})=\tilde{\gamma}^{j}\}\right).

By construction, if
I\!\!P(A)
is non null, then the sets
\{(\hat{\gamma}^{1}_{s},\hat{\ell}^{1}_{s},s),s\in\mathbb{Z},\hat{\gamma}^{1}_{s}\notin\{0,\infty\}\}
and
\{(\hat{\gamma}^{2}_{s},\hat{\ell}^{2}_{s},s),s\in\mathbb{Z},\hat{\gamma}^{2}_{s}\notin\{0,\infty\}\}
are disjoint from the sets
\{(\tilde{\gamma}^{j}_{s},\tilde{\ell}^{j}_{s},s),s\in\mathbb{Z},\tilde{\gamma}^{j}_{s}\notin\{0,\infty\}\}
and
\{(\gamma^{j}_{s},0,s),s\in\mathbb{Z},\gamma^{j}_{s}\notin\{0,\infty\}\}
for
j=3,4
so that the two events in the final expression of
A
are independent, and Item (ii) follows.

Proof of (iii)

Let
\hat{\gamma}^{1},\tilde{\gamma}^{2}\in\{0,1,\dots,N,\infty\}^{\mathbb{Z}},
and let
\hat{\ell}^{1}\in\{0,5\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\tilde{\ell}^{2}\in\{0,2\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be two fixed layer processes that are compatible with the event
\{\hat{I}^{z_{1}}=\hat{\gamma}^{1}\}\cap\{\tilde{I}^{z_{2}}=\tilde{\gamma}^{2}\}
. By compatible, we mean for example that supposing that
\hat{\gamma}^{1}
and
\tilde{\gamma}^{2}
meet at some time
s\leq t_{1}\vee t_{2},
it is not possible to have
\hat{l}^{1}_{u}=\tilde{l}^{2}_{u}
for
u\leq s.

Then, analogously to the proof of point (ii) above, the sets
\{(\hat{\gamma}^{1}_{s},\hat{\ell}^{1}_{s},s),s\in\mathbb{Z},\hat{\gamma}_{s}^{1}\notin\{0,\infty\}\}
and
\{(\tilde{\gamma}^{2}_{s},\tilde{\ell}^{2}_{s},s),s\in\mathbb{Z},\tilde{\gamma}_{s}^{2}\notin\{0,\infty\}\}
are disjoint sets implying the independence.
∎

Proposition B.6
.

For all
i
,
\tilde{I}^{z_{i}}
has the same distribution as
I^{z_{i}}
and
(\hat{I}^{z_{1}},\hat{I}^{z_{2}})
has the same distribution as
(I^{z_{1}},I^{z_{2}})
.

Remind that the layers
J^{(0)},\dots,J^{(5)}
are i.i.d. so that it is easy to prove that
\tilde{I}^{z_{i}}
is a backward random walk with the same transitions as
I^{z_{i}}
(compare Equation (
40
) with Equation (
28
)) which in turn implies that they share the same distribution.

We now turn to the second part of the proof. For the marginals, the same argument as above applies (thanks to Equation (
42
)). Hence,
\hat{I}^{z_{i}}
has the same distribution as
I^{z_{i}}
. Then, we show that the process
(\hat{I}^{z_{1}}_{s},\hat{I}^{z_{2}}_{s})_{s}
is a two-dimensional Markov chain (backwards in time) which has the same transitions as
(I^{z_{1}}_{s},I^{z_{2}}_{s})_{s}.
Since both chains start from the same initial conditions, this implies the desired result.

Since transitions between
t_{1}\vee t_{2}
and
t_{1}\wedge t_{2}
only concern one of the two processes, evolving according to the right marginals, we do only need to
consider transitions
s\to s-1
for
s\leq t_{1}\wedge t_{2}.
Fix
i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2}\in\{0,\ldots,N\}.
Let us first discuss the case
i_{1}\neq i_{2}.

Then for any
l_{1},l_{2}\in\{0,5\},
writing

A:=\{\hat{I}^{z_{1}}_{s}=i_{1},\hat{I}^{z_{2}}_{s}=i_{2},\hat{L}^{z_{1}}_{s}=l_{1},\hat{L}^{z_{2}}_{s}=l_{2}\},

we have that

I\!\!P(\hat{I}^{z_{1}}_{s-1}=j_{1},\hat{I}^{z_{2}}_{s-1}=j_{2}|A)=I\!\!P(J^{(l_{1})}_{(i_{1},s)}=j_{1},J^{(l_{2})}_{(i_{2},s)}=j_{2}|A).

By construction,
J^{(l_{1})}_{(i_{1},s)}
and
J^{(l_{2})}_{(i_{2},s)}
are independent (since
i_{1}\neq i_{2}),
they have the same joint distribution as
(J^{(0)}_{(i_{1},s)},J^{(0)}_{(i_{2},s)}),
and they are independent of
A
(since
A
depends only on decisions strictly after time
s
). Thus

I\!\!P(\hat{I}^{z_{1}}_{s-1}=j_{1},\hat{I}^{z_{2}}_{s-1}=j_{2}|A)=I\!\!P(J^{(0)}_{(i_{1},s)}=j_{1},J^{(0)}_{(i_{2},s)}=j_{2}).

Summing over all possible choices of
l_{1}
and
l_{2},
this implies that

I\!\!P(\hat{I}^{z_{1}}_{s-1}=j_{1},\hat{I}^{z_{2}}_{s-1}=j_{2}|\hat{I}^{z_{1}}_{s}=i_{1},\hat{I}^{z_{2}}_{s}=i_{2})=I\!\!P(J^{(0)}_{(i_{1},s)}=j_{1},J^{(0)}_{(i_{2},s)}=j_{2}).

(44)

We now discuss the case
i_{1}=i_{2}.
Then necessarily
l_{1}=l_{2}
and thus, by our coalescence construction,
j_{1}=j_{2}.
With the same notation for the set
A
as above and using the same independence argument, it still holds that

I\!\!P(\hat{I}^{z_{1}}_{s-1}=j_{1}=\hat{I}^{z_{2}}_{s-1}|A)=I\!\!P(J^{(l_{1})}_{(i_{1},s)}=j_{1}|A)=I\!\!P(J^{(l_{1})}_{(i_{1},s)}=j_{1})=I\!\!P(J^{(0)}_{(i_{1},s)}=j_{1}).

Summing over all possible values of
l_{1}
implies that

I\!\!P(\hat{I}^{z_{1}}_{s-1}=j_{1}=\hat{I}^{z_{2}}_{s-1}|\hat{I}^{z_{1}}_{s}=i_{1}=\hat{I}^{z_{2}}_{s})=I\!\!P(J^{(0)}_{(i_{1},s)}=j_{1}).

(45)

The transition probabilities given by Equations (
44
) and (
45
) correspond exactly to the transition probabilities of
(I^{z_{1}}_{s},I^{z_{2}}_{s})
- see Equation (
28
).
∎

B.4
Proof of the two lemmas

Four tilde processes are constructed above so we write the proof for
k=4
. Nevertheless, the proof can easily be generalized to any
k\geq 2
.

Let
z_{1},\dots,z_{4}
be four different sites in
\mathcal{Z}
. For all
i=1,\dots,4
, let
\tilde{I}^{z_{i}}
be the backward random walks defined by (
39
), and
\tilde{L}^{z_{i}}
be the associated layer process. We denote
\tilde{L}^{z_{i}}_{-\infty}=\lim_{s\to-\infty}\tilde{L}^{z_{i}}_{s}\in\{0,i\}
the terminal layer. Then, remind the functions
g
and
r
defined in the beginning of Appendix
B.2
and define, for all
i=1,\dots,4
,

\tilde{X}_{z_{i}}=g\left(\tilde{I}^{z_{i}},\xi^{(0)}_{r(\tilde{I}^{z_{i}})}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\tilde{L}^{z_{i}}_{-\infty}=0}+g\left(\tilde{I}^{z_{i}},\xi^{(i)}_{r(\tilde{I}^{z_{i}})}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\tilde{L}^{z_{i}}_{-\infty}=i}.

In comparison, remind that
X_{z_{i}}=g(I^{z_{i}},\xi^{(0)}_{r(I^{z_{i}})})
. Using the fact that the
\xi^{(j)}
’s are i.i.d. with Propositions
B.5
and
B.6
, one deduces Items (i) and (ii). Finally, Item (iii) follows from the fact that
\{\tilde{X}^{z_{i}}\neq X^{z_{i}}\}\subset\{\tilde{I}^{z_{i}}\neq I^{z_{i}}\}
and Proposition
B.4
.
∎

Let
z_{1},\dots,z_{4}
be four different sites in
\mathcal{Z}
. For all
i=1,\dots,4
, let us define
\tilde{X}_{z_{i}}
as in the proof of Lemma
B.1
. In particular, Items (iv) to (vi) follow from Lemma
B.1
.

Then, for
i=1,2
, let
\hat{I}^{z_{i}}
be the backward random walks defined by (
41
), and
\hat{L}^{z_{i}}
be the associated layer process. We denote
\hat{L}^{z_{i}}_{-\infty}=\lim_{s\to-\infty}\tilde{L}^{z_{i}}_{s}\in\{0,5\}
the terminal layer. Then, remind the functions
g
and
r
defined in the beginning of Appendix
B.2
and define, for all
i=1,\dots,4
,

\hat{X}_{z_{i}}=g\left(\hat{I}^{z_{i}},\xi^{(0)}_{r(\hat{I}^{z_{i}})}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\hat{L}^{z_{i}}_{-\infty}=0}+g\left(\hat{I}^{z_{i}},\xi^{(5)}_{r(\hat{I}^{z_{i}})}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\hat{L}^{z_{i}}_{-\infty}=5}.

Using the fact that the
\xi^{(j)}
’s are i.i.d., Item (i) follows from Proposition
B.6
, Items (ii) and (v) follow respectively from Items (ii) and (iii) of Proposition
B.5
. Finally, Items (ii) and (iii) follow from Item (ii) of Proposition
B.4
.
∎

C
Coalescence of two or more backward random walks

The aim of this section is to prove Proposition
3.5
. Throughout the proof we will consider partitions of the sets of cardinal 2, 3 and 4. For a finite set
E
, we denote by
\mathcal{P}(E)
the set of its partitions. For ease of notation, we consider a notation which we exemplify in the case of a set
\{a,b,c\}
with three different elements. In this case, the set
\mathcal{P}(\{a,b,c\})
has 5 elements which are :

•

\{\{a\},\{b\},\{c\}\}
written as
a\!\dagger\!b\!\dagger\!c
,

•

\{\{a,b\},\{c\}\}
, written as
ab\!\dagger\!c
,

•

\{\{a,c\},\{b\}\}
, written as
ac\!\dagger\!b
,

•

\{\{a\},\{b,c\}\}
, written as
a\!\dagger\!bc
,

•

\{\{a,b,c\}\}
, written as
abc
.

The proof relies on the Markovian property of the backward random walks
(I^{z})_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}
. From these backward random walks (BRW), we define a “status” random walk - such as
S^{1}_{t}
below - which informs us whether the BRW has started, is currently running, or has already reached the cemetery. This, in turn, allows us to define a partition valued Markov chain, denoted
P_{t}
. To keep concise notation for the states of this partition valued Markov chain, we use the notation introduced above, which informs us on the coalescing status of the BRWs.
The proof is then based on the computation of the transition probabilities of the partition Markov chain (some transition graphs are drawn for pedagogical purposes). Finally, the proof is concluded by the computation of the coalescence probabilities.

Without loss of generality, we assume in this proof that
t_{1}\geq t_{2}\geq t_{3}\geq t_{4}
.

Proof of Item (i)

Consider the backward random walks
I^{z_{1}}
and
I^{z_{2}}
from time
t=t_{1}
to time
t=-\infty
. Let us introduce the following notation: in what follows, the letter ‘
S
’ stands for the state of a random walk;
S^{1}_{t}
will be the state at time
t
of the random walk associated to
z_{1},
and
S^{2}_{t}
the state at time
t
of the random walk associated to
z_{2}
. We define
S^{1}_{t}=1_{\infty}
if
t>t_{1},

S^{1}_{t}=1_{0}
if
I^{z_{1}}_{t}=0
,
S^{1}_{t}=1
else, and by analogy,
S^{2}_{t}=2_{\infty}
if
t>t_{2},

S^{2}_{t}=2_{0}
if
I^{z_{2}}_{t}=0,

S^{2}_{t}=2
else.

In what follows, we extend the notation introduced above for partitions of sets. Let us denote
P_{t}=S^{1}_{t}\!\dagger\!S^{2}_{t}
if
t>\tau^{c}_{z_{1},z_{2}}
and
P_{t}=S^{1}_{t}S^{2}_{t}
else. Then, the backward process
(P_{t})_{-\infty<t\leq t_{1}}
is a time in-homogeneous backward Markov chain on the state space
\mathcal{P}_{2}=\cup_{a\in\{1_{\infty},1,1_{0}\}}\cup_{b\in\{2_{\infty},2,2_{0}\}}\{ab,a\!\dagger\!b\}
. At time
t=t_{1}
, the backward process starts from the initial condition
P_{t_{1}}=1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}
if
t_{1}>t_{2},
and
P_{t_{1}}=1\!\dagger\!2
if
t_{1}=t_{2}
(note that in that case
z_{1}\neq z_{2}
implies that
i_{1}\neq i_{2}
so that
P_{t_{1}}=12
is not possible).
By Proposition
3.1
, the random variable
\min\{\tau^{R}_{z_{1}},\tau^{R}_{z_{2}}\}>-\infty
almost surely, implying that
P_{-\infty}:=\lim_{t\to-\infty}P_{t}
exists almost surely and
P_{-\infty}\in\{1_{0}2_{0},1_{0}\!\dagger\!2_{0}\}
, that is the set of absorbing states.
One can check that

I\!\!P_{\theta}(\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{2}\})=I\!\!P_{\theta}\left(P_{-\infty}=1_{0}2_{0}\,|\,P_{t_{1}}=1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}\right),

(46)

in case
t_{1}>t_{2},
and

I\!\!P_{\theta}(\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{2}\})=I\!\!P_{\theta}(P_{-\infty}=1_{0}2_{0}\,|\,P_{t_{1}}=1\!\dagger\!2),

(47)

in case
t_{1}=t_{2}.

Now, for
t\leq t_{1}
, let us denote
Q_{t}(x,y)
the transition probabilities of the time in-homogeneous backward Markov chain
(P_{t})_{-\infty<t\leq t_{1}}
:

Q_{t}(x,y)=I\!\!P_{\theta}\left(P_{t-1}=y|P_{t}=x\right),\;x,y\in\mathcal{P}_{2}.

Assume for now that
t_{1}>t_{2}
. Since we are only interested in computing the coalescence probability (
46
), we only need to give the transition probabilities encountered on the path from state
1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}
to state
1_{0}2_{0}
.
For
t>t_{2}+1
, the only relevant transition probability is
Q_{t}(1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty},1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty})=1-\lambda
. At time
t=t_{2}+1
there are two relevant transitions:
Q_{t_{2}+1}(1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}
,
1\!\dagger\!2)=(1-\lambda)(1-1/N)
and
Q_{t_{2}+1}(1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty},12)=(1-\lambda)/N
.

For
t\leq t_{2}
, there are four relevant transition probabilities:

•

Q_{t}(1\!\dagger\!2,1\!\dagger\!2)=(1-\lambda)^{2}(1-1/2)

•

Q_{t}(1\!\dagger\!2,12)=(1-\lambda)^{2}/N
,

•

Q_{t}(12,1_{0}2_{0})=1-Q_{t}(12,12)=\lambda
.

Figure
3
gives a graphical representation of the relevant transitions just described in case
t_{2}<t_{1}
.

1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}
1\!\dagger\!2
12
1_{0}2_{0}
1-\lambda
(1-\lambda)(1-1/N)
\frac{1-\lambda}{N}
(1-\lambda)^{2}(1-1/N)
\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{N}
1-\lambda
\lambda
1

Fig 3:
Graph of the relevant transitions of the backward process
P
used to compute a bound for
I\!\!P_{\theta}(\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{2}\})
when
t_{1}\geq t_{2}
. The starting node is
1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}
if
t_{1}>t_{2}
and
1\!\dagger\!2
else. On each edge, the corresponding transition probability is given. The gray vertical line separates two temporal zones: the right one corresponds to times
t>t_{2}+1
and the left one corresponds to times
t\leq t_{2}+1
.

Starting from Equation (
46
), one can check that

I\!\!P_{\theta}(\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{2}\})=I\!\!P_{\theta}(P_{t_{2}}=12|P_{t_{1}}=1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty})I\!\!P_{\theta}(P_{-\infty}=1_{0}2_{0}|P_{t_{2}}=12)\\
+I\!\!P_{\theta}(P_{t_{2}}=1\!\dagger\!2|P_{t_{1}}=1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty})I\!\!P_{\theta}(P_{-\infty}=1_{0}2_{0}|P_{t_{2}}=1\!\dagger\!2).

Now, by the dynamics of the Markov chain
(P_{t})_{t\leq t_{1}}
we have that
I\!\!P_{\theta}(P_{t_{2}}=1\!\dagger\!2|P_{t_{1}}=1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty})=(1-\lambda)^{t_{1}-t_{2}}
and
I\!\!P_{\theta}(P_{t_{2}}=12|P_{t_{1}}=1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty})=(1-\lambda)^{t_{1}-t_{2}}/N
. Moreover,
I\!\!P_{\theta}(P_{-\infty}=1_{0}2_{0}|P_{t_{2}}=12)=1
by Proposition
3.1
. By observing that

\displaystyle I\!\!P_{\theta}(P_{-\infty}=1_{0}2_{0}|P_{t_{2}}=1\!\dagger\!2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{N}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(1-\lambda)^{2k}(1-1/N)^{k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{N}\frac{1}{1-(1-\lambda)^{2}},

and by putting all pieces together, we then obtain that

\displaystyle I\!\!P_{\theta}(\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{2}\})\leq\frac{(1-\lambda)^{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}}{N}\left[1+\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{{1-(1-\lambda)^{2}}}\right]=\frac{(1-\lambda)^{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}}{N(1-(1-\lambda)^{2})}.

(48)

In the case
t_{2}=t_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle I\!\!P_{\theta}(\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{2}\})=I\!\!P_{\theta}(P_{-\infty}=1_{0}2_{0}|P_{t_{2}}=1\!\dagger\!2)\\
\leq\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{N}\frac{1}{1-(1-\lambda)^{2}}\leq\frac{(1-\lambda)}{N(1-(1-\lambda)^{2})},

(49)

so that Item (i) follows from inequalities (
48
) and (
49
).

Proof of Item (ii)

By analogy to the case with two sites, we define
S^{3}_{t}=3_{\infty}
if
t>t_{3},

S^{3}_{t}=3_{0}
if
I^{z_{3}}_{t}=0
,
S^{3}_{t}=3
else.
Let us describe the backward process
(P_{t})_{-\infty<t\leq t_{1}}
in that case. First note that for all
t\leq t_{1}
,
P_{t}
is a partition of
\{S^{1}_{t},S^{2}_{t},S^{3}_{t}\}
and in particular
P_{s}\in\mathcal{P}_{3}=\cup_{a\in\{1_{\infty},1,1_{0}\}}\cup_{b\in\{2_{\infty},2,2_{0}\}}\cup_{c\in\{3_{\infty},3,3_{0}\}}\mathcal{P}(\{a,b,c\})
. The choice of the partition is induced by the equivalence relation defined by

S^{k}_{t}\leftrightarrow_{t}S^{\ell}_{t}\mbox{ if and only if }t\leq\tau^{c}_{z_{k},z_{\ell}}.

This equivalence relation naturally induces a partition of
\{S^{1}_{t},S^{2}_{t},S^{3}_{t}\}
and we define
P_{t}
as this partition. Similarly to the proof of Item (i), we have

I\!\!P_{\theta}(\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{2}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{3}\})=I\!\!P_{\theta}\left(P_{-\infty}=1_{0}2_{0}3_{0}\,|\,P_{t_{1}}=1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}\right),

(50)

in case
t_{1}>t_{2}\geq t_{3}
for instance, where

P_{-\infty}=\lim_{t\to-\infty}P_{t}
exists almost surely and
P_{-\infty}\in{\cal{P}}(\{1_{0},2_{0},3_{0}\}).

Assume for now that
t_{1}>t_{2}>t_{3}
which is the most general case. The other cases can be treated similarly.

For
t\leq t_{1}
, let us denote the transition probabilities of the chain

Q_{t}(x,y)=I\!\!P_{\theta}\left(P_{t-1}=y|P_{t}=x\right),\;x,y\in\mathcal{P}_{3}.

Since we are only interested in computing the coalescence probability (
50
), we only need to give the transition probabilities encountered on the path from state
1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}
to state
1_{0}2_{0}3_{0}
.
For
t>t_{2}+1
, the only relevant transition probability is
Q_{t}(1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}\!\dagger\!3_{\infty},1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}\!\dagger\!3_{\infty})=1-\lambda
. At time
t=t_{2}+1
, there are two relevant transition probabilities:
Q_{t_{2}+1}(1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}\!\dagger\!3_{\infty},1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3_{\infty})=(1-\lambda)(1-1/N)
and
Q_{t_{2}+1}(1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}\!\dagger\!3_{\infty},12\!\dagger\!3_{\infty})=(1-\lambda)/(N)
.

For
t_{3}+1<t\leq t_{2}
the three relevant transition probabilities are :

•

Q_{t}(1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3_{\infty},1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3_{\infty})=(1-\lambda)^{2}(1-1/N)\leq(1-\lambda)^{2}
,

•

Q_{t}(1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3_{\infty},12\!\dagger\!3_{\infty})=(1-\lambda)^{2}/N\leq(1-\lambda)N^{-1}
,

•

Q_{t}(12\!\dagger\!3_{\infty},12\!\dagger\!3_{\infty})=(1-\lambda)
.

At time
t=t_{3}+1
, the seven relevant transition probabilities are :

•

Q_{t}(1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3_{\infty},1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3)=(1-\lambda)^{2}(1-1/N)(1-2/N)\leq(1-\lambda)^{2}
,

•

Q_{t}(1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3_{\infty},1\!\dagger\!23)=Q_{t}(1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3_{\infty},13\!\dagger\!2)=Q_{t}(1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3_{\infty},12\!\dagger\!3)\leq(1-\lambda)^{2}N^{-1}
,

•

Q_{t}(1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3_{\infty},123)=(1-\lambda)^{2}/N^{2}
.

•

Q_{t}(12\!\dagger\!3_{\infty},12\!\dagger\!3)=(1-\lambda)(1-/N)\leq(1-\lambda),

•

Q_{t}(12\!\dagger\!3_{\infty},123)=(1-\lambda)/N
.

Finally, for
t\leq t_{3}
, the thirteen relevant transition probabilities are :

•

Q_{t}(1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3,1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3)\leq(1-\lambda)^{3}
,

•

Q_{t}(1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3,12\!\dagger\!3)=Q_{t}(1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3,1\!\dagger\!23)=Q_{t}(1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3,2\!\dagger\!13)\leq(1-\lambda)^{3}N^{-1}
,

•

Q_{t}(1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3,123)\leq(1-\lambda)^{3}N^{-2}
,

•

Q_{t}(12\!\dagger\!3,12\!\dagger\!3)=Q_{t}(1\!\dagger\!23,1\!\dagger\!23)=Q_{t}(2\!\dagger\!13,2\!\dagger\!13)\leq(1-\lambda)^{2}
,

•

Q_{t}(12\!\dagger\!3,123)=Q_{t}(1\!\dagger\!23,123)=Q_{t}(2\!\dagger\!13,123)\leq(1-\lambda)^{2}N^{-1}
,

•

Q_{t}(123,1_{0}2_{0}3_{0})=1-Q_{t}(123,123)=\lambda
.

1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}
1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}
12\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}
1-\lambda
1-\lambda
\frac{1-\lambda}{N}
(1-\lambda)^{2}
\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{N}
1-\lambda

(for times

) used to compute a bound for

when

. The starting node is

if
t_{1}>t_{2}>3
,
1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}
if
t_{1}=t_{2}>t_{3}
and
1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3
(which appears in Figure
5
) else. On each edge, an upper bound of the corresponding transition probability is given. The gray vertical line separates two temporal zones: zone II corresponds to times
t_{3}+1<t\leq t_{2}+1
and zone III corresponds to times
t>t_{2}+1
.

1

Fig 5:
Graph of the relevant transitions of the backward process
P
(for times
t\leq t_{3}+1
) used to compute a bound for
I\!\!P_{\theta}(\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{2}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{3}\})
when
t_{1}\geq t_{2}\geq t_{3}
. The starting node is
1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}
(which appears in Figure
4
) if
t_{1}>t_{2}>3
,
1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}
if
t_{1}=t_{2}>t_{3}
and
1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3
else. On some of the edges, an upper bound of the corresponding transition probability is given (the nodes
1\!\dagger\!23
,
13\!\dagger\!2
and
12\!\dagger\!3
play almost the same role that is why we omit some the transition probabilities). The gray vertical line separates two temporal zones: zone I corresponds to times
t\leq t_{3}+1
and zone II corresponds to times
t_{3}+1<t\leq t_{2}+1
.

Looking at the Figures
4
and
5
, the Markov property of
(P_{t})_{t\leq t_{1}}
permits to compute the probabilities of ending in state
1_{0}2_{0}3_{0}
from any of the other states of the graph. These two figures highlight three time zones denoted I, II and III, which are depicted from left to right corresponding to the forward evolution of time (hence the arrows go from right to left since they correspond to a backward dynamics).

In the following, we decompose the computation of the probability in Equation (
50
) with respect to the three zones. To simplify the notation, let us denote for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\Theta\in\mathcal{P}_{3}
,
p(t,\Theta)=I\!\!P_{\theta}\left(P_{-\infty}=1_{0}2_{0}3_{0}\,|\,P_{t}=\Theta\right)
.

For zone I, we have to consider initial conditions starting at time
t_{3}
(see Figure
5
). That is,

p(t_{3},1\!\dagger\!23)=p(t_{3},12\!\dagger\!3)=p(t_{3},13\!\dagger\!2)\leq K(1-\lambda)^{2}N^{-1},

and

p(t_{3},1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3)\leq K(1-\lambda)^{3}\left[3p(t_{3},1\!\dagger\!23)N^{-1}+N^{-2}\right]\leq K(1-\lambda)^{3}N^{-2}.

For zone II, we have for all
t_{3}<t\leq t_{2}
,

p(t,12\!\dagger\!3_{\infty})=(1-\lambda)^{t-t_{3}}[p(t_{3},12\!\dagger\!3)+N^{-1}p(t_{3},123)]\leq K(1-\lambda)^{t-t_{3}}N^{-1},

since we have to wait in the middle part during
t-t_{3}-1
steps and then take the edge from
12\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}
to
12\!\dagger\!3
or the edge from
12\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}
to
123
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle p(t_{2},1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3_{\infty})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle K\left\{\sum_{k=1}^{t_{2}-t_{3}-1}(1-\lambda)^{2k}p(t_{2}-k,12\!\dagger\!3_{\infty})N^{-1}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+(1-\lambda)^{2(t_{2}-t_{3})}\left[3p(t_{3},1\!\dagger\!23)N^{-1}+p(t_{3},1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3)\right]\right\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle K\left\{(1-\lambda)^{t_{2}-t_{3}}\sum_{k=1}^{t_{2}-t_{3}-1}(1-\lambda)^{k}N^{-2}+4(1-\lambda)^{2(t_{2}-t_{3})}(1-\lambda)^{2}N^{-2}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle K(1-\lambda)^{t_{2}-t_{3}}N^{-2}.

Finally, using zone III, we have

\displaystyle I\!\!P_{\theta}(\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{2}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{3}\})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p(t_{1},1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}\!\dagger\!3_{\infty})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\lambda)^{t_{1}-t_{2}}[p(t_{2},1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3_{\infty})(1-1/2N)+p(t_{2},12\!\dagger\!3_{\infty})(2N)^{-1}],

which ends the proof.

Proof of Item (iii)

The backward process
(P_{t})_{-\infty<t\leq t_{1}}
is naturally extended to the case of four sites and we use the decomposition

\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{2}\}\cap\{z_{3}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{4}\}=\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{2}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{3}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{4}\}\cup\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{2}\not\leftrightsquigarrow z_{3}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{4}\}.

Assume for now that
t_{1}>t_{2}\geq t_{3}\geq t_{4}
so that
P_{t_{1}}=1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}\!\dagger\!4_{\infty}.
It suffices to control

I\!\!P_{\theta}\left(P_{-\infty}=1_{0}2_{0}3_{0}4_{0}\,|\,P_{t_{1}}=1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}\!\dagger\!4_{\infty}\right)\\
+I\!\!P_{\theta}\left(P_{-\infty}=1_{0}2_{0}\!\dagger\!3_{0}4_{0}\,|\,P_{t_{1}}=1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}\!\dagger\!4_{\infty}\right).

The list of the relevant transitions and the corresponding graphs are not written here due to their huge size. However the computations can be made in the same manner.
For instance, one can prove that

I\!\!P_{\theta}\left(P_{-\infty}=1_{0}2_{0}3_{0}4_{0}\,|\,P_{t_{1}}=1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}\!\dagger\!4_{\infty}\right)\leq K(1-\lambda)^{t_{1}-t_{4}}N^{-3},

and

I\!\!P_{\theta}\left(P_{-\infty}=1_{0}2_{0}\!\dagger\!3_{0}4_{0}\,|\,P_{t_{1}}=1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}\!\dagger\!4_{\infty}\right)\leq K(1-\lambda)^{(t_{1}-t_{2})+(t_{3}-t_{4})}N^{-2},

which in turn give the desired result.
Finally, the same upper bound holds if
t_{1}>t_{2}
is not satisfied by replacing the initial condition (for instance
t_{1}=t_{2}>t_{3}\geq t_{4}
corresponds to the initial condition
P_{t_{1}}=1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}\!\dagger\!4_{\infty}
if
z_{1}\neq z_{2}
).

Proof of Item (iv)

We will only prove the case
t_{1}\wedge t_{2}>t_{3}\vee t_{4}
. The case in which
t_{1}\wedge t_{2}=t_{3}\vee t_{4}
follows along the same lines. In the sequel, as before,
we assume without loss of generality that
t_{1}\geq t_{2}>t_{3}\geq t_{4}
(the case where
t_{1}<t_{2}
can be treated in the same way). In the following, we use the backward process
(P_{t})_{-\infty<t\leq t_{1}}
with four sites like in the proof of Item (iii). Then, since
t_{3}+1\leq t_{2},

\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{3}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{4}\not\leftrightsquigarrow z_{2}\}\cap\{\tau^{R}_{z_{2}}<t_{3}\}\subset\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{3}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{4}\not\leftrightsquigarrow z_{2}\}\cap\{\tau^{R}_{z_{2}}<t_{3}+1\}\\
=\left\{P_{-\infty}=1_{0}3_{0}4_{0}\!\dagger\!2_{0}\right\}\cap\{P_{t_{3}+1}=1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}\!\dagger\!4_{\infty}\}.

Assume for now that
t_{1}>t_{2}
. It follows from the same arguments as above that

I\!\!P_{\theta}\left(P_{-\infty}=1_{0}3_{0}4_{0}\!\dagger\!2_{0}\,|\,P_{t_{3}+1}=1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}\!\dagger\!4_{\infty}\right)\leq K(1-\lambda)^{t_{3}+1-t_{4}}N^{-2},

and

I\!\!P_{\theta}\left(P_{t_{3}+1}=1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}\!\dagger\!4_{\infty}\,|\,P_{t_{1}}=1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}\!\dagger\!4_{\infty}\right)\leq(1-\lambda)^{t_{1}-(t_{3}+1)+t_{2}-(t_{3}+1)}.

which in turn give the desired result.

Finally, if
t_{1}=t_{2}
, the same upper-bound holds with the initial condition
P_{t_{1}}=1\!\dagger\!2_{\infty}\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}\!\dagger\!4_{\infty}
replaced by
P_{t_{1}}=1\!\dagger\!2\!\dagger\!3_{\infty}\!\dagger\!4_{\infty}
.
∎

D
Proof of Lemma
3.8

The statements in both Items 1 and 2 are trivial, so that we only need to prove Items 3 through 7. Items 3, 4 and 5 are proved in a similar way: Lemma
B.1
is used to relate independence with the high probability event of no coalescence, and some easy computations are needed to bound the covariance. Items 6 and 7 use the more involved Lemma
B.2
.

In this case,
B=\cov_{\theta}[Y^{2}_{z_{1}},Y^{2}_{z_{3}}]
. Let
\tilde{X}_{z_{1}}
be a random variable distributed as
X_{z_{1}}
, independent of
X_{z_{3}}

and such that
\{X_{z_{1}}\neq\tilde{X}_{z_{1}}\}\subset\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{3}\}
.
The existence of such random variables is ensured by Lemma
B.1
with
k=2
.
In particular, we have that
I\!\!E_{\theta}[\tilde{X}_{z_{1}}]=I\!\!E_{\theta}[X_{z_{1}}]
. Denote
\tilde{Y}_{z_{1}}=\tilde{X}_{z_{1}}-I\!\!E_{\theta}[\tilde{X}_{z_{1}}]
and observe that
\{\tilde{Y}_{z_{1}}\neq Y_{z_{1}}\}=\{\tilde{X}_{z_{1}}\neq X_{z_{1}}\}
. By using the properties satisfied by random variables
\tilde{X}_{z_{1}},

one can check that
\tilde{Y}_{z_{1}}

is independent of
Y_{z_{3}}
,
so that

B=\cov_{\theta}[Y^{2}_{z_{1}},Y^{2}_{z_{3}}]=I\!\!E_{\theta}[(Y^{2}_{z_{1}}-\tilde{Y}^{2}_{z_{1}})Y^{2}_{z_{3}}].

Since
\{\tilde{Y}_{z_{1}}\neq Y_{z_{1}}\}=\{\tilde{X}_{z_{1}}\neq X_{z_{1}}\}\subset\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{3}\}
and
\max\{|Y_{z_{1}}|,|\tilde{Y}_{z_{1}}|,|Y_{z_{3}}|\}\leq 1
almost surely, it follows that

|B|\leq I\!\!P_{\theta}\left(z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{3}\right),

so that the result follows Item (i) of Proposition
3.5
.
∎

Suppose that
z_{1}=z_{2}.
Then
B=\cov_{\theta}[Y^{2}_{z_{1}},Y_{z_{3}}Y_{z_{4}}]
.
By Lemma
B.1
with
k=3
, there exist random variables
\tilde{Y}_{z_{1}},\tilde{Y}_{z_{3}}
and
\tilde{Y}_{z_{4}}
defined in such a way that the following properties hold for each
i\in\{1,3,4\}
: 1)
\tilde{Y}_{z_{i}}
has the same law as
Y_{z_{i}}
; 2)

\tilde{Y}_{z_{i}}
is independent of
\tilde{Y}_{z_{j}}
and
Y_{z_{j}}
for all
j\in\{1,3,4\}\setminus\{i\}
; 3)
\{\tilde{Y}_{z_{i}}\neq Y_{z_{i}}\}\subset\cup_{j\in\{1,3,4\}\setminus\{i\}}\{z_{i}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{j}\}.

Using first that
Y^{2}_{z_{1}}
has same law as
\tilde{Y}^{2}_{z_{1}}
and then that
I\!\!E_{\theta}[\tilde{Y}_{z_{i}}]=I\!\!E_{\theta}[Y_{z_{i}}]=0
, we can deduce from properties 1 and 2 above that

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I\!\!E_{\theta}[(Y^{2}_{z_{1}}-\tilde{Y}^{2}_{z_{1}})Y_{z_{3}}Y_{z_{4}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I\!\!E_{\theta}[(Y^{2}_{z_{1}}-\tilde{Y}^{2}_{z_{1}})(Y_{z_{3}}-\tilde{Y}_{z_{3}})(Y_{z_{4}}-\tilde{Y}_{z_{4}})].

By combining property 3, the above identity and the fact that
\leftrightsquigarrow
is an equivalence relation, we can deduce that

|B|\leq I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[1_{\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{3}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{4}\}}\right]=I\!\!P_{\theta}(\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{2}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{3}\}),

so that the result follows from Item (ii) of Proposition
3.5
.
∎

Let us consider the case in which
z_{1}=z_{3}
. Let
z_{1},z_{2},z_{4}
be three different sites. The goal is to bound the covariance

B=\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}},Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{4}}\right]=I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[Y^{2}_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}Y_{z_{4}}\right]-\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{1}},Y_{z_{2}}\right]\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{1}},Y_{z_{4}}\right].

(51)

Arguing exactly like in the proof of Item 3, one can show that

\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{1}},Y_{z_{2}}\right]\leq K(1-\lambda)^{|t_{1}-t_{2}|}N^{-1}
and
\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{1}},Y_{z_{4}}\right]\leq K(1-\lambda)^{|t_{1}-t_{4}|}N^{-1}
, so that

\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{1}},Y_{z_{2}}\right]\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{1}},Y_{z_{4}}\right]\leq K(1-\lambda)^{|t_{1}-t_{2}|+|t_{1}-t_{4}|}N^{-2}.

(52)

We will now deal with the term
I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[Y^{2}_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}Y_{z_{4}}\right].
For that end, let
\tilde{X}_{z_{1}}
,
\tilde{X}_{z_{2}}
and
\tilde{X}_{z_{4}}
be the random variables defined in Lemma
B.1
with
k=3
, and denote
\tilde{Y}_{z_{i}}=\tilde{X}_{z_{i}}-I\!\!E_{\theta}(X_{z_{i}})
,
i\in\{1,2,4\}
the corresponding centered versions. By using the independence properties of the random variables
\tilde{Y}_{z_{i}}
(and the fact that they are all centered), one can check that

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[Y^{2}_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}Y_{z_{4}}\right]=I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[(Y^{2}_{z_{1}}-\tilde{Y}^{2}_{z_{1}})(Y_{z_{2}}-\tilde{Y}_{z_{2}})(Y_{z_{4}}-\tilde{Y}_{z_{4}})\right]+I\!\!E_{\theta}[\tilde{Y}^{2}_{z_{1}}]\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{2}},Y_{z_{4}}\right].

Arguing exactly like in the proof of Item 4, one can deduce that

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[(Y^{2}_{z_{1}}-\tilde{Y}^{2}_{z_{1}})(Y_{z_{2}}-\tilde{Y}_{z_{2}})(Y_{z_{4}}-\tilde{Y}_{z_{4}})\right]\leq K(1-\lambda)^{|s_{3}-s_{2}|+|s_{2}-s_{1}|}N^{-2},

(53)

where
s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq s_{3}
is an ordering of
\{t_{1},t_{2},t_{4}\}
.
Finally, since
\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{2}},Y_{z_{4}}\right]\leq K(1-\lambda)^{|t_{2}-t_{4}|}N^{-1}
, the random variables
\tilde{Y}_{z_{k}}
are bounded by 1 almost surely and
|t_{2}-t_{4}|\leq\min\{|t_{2}-t_{1}|+|t_{1}-t_{4}|,|s_{3}-s_{2}|+|s_{2}-s_{1}|\}
, it follows from (
51
), (
52
) and (
53
) that

|B|\leq K(1-\lambda)^{|t_{2}-t_{4}|}N^{-1},

concluding the proof.
∎

Let
z_{1},\dots,z_{4}
be four different points in
\cal Z
and denote by
\hat{X}_{z_{1}},\hat{X}_{z_{2}}
,
\tilde{X}_{z_{1}},\ldots,\tilde{X}_{z_{4}}
the random variables defined in Lemma
B.2
. Moreover, denote by
\tilde{Y}_{z_{k}}=\tilde{X}_{z_{k}}-I\!\!E_{\theta}(\tilde{X}_{z_{k}})
,
1\leq k\leq 4
and
\hat{Y}_{z_{k}}=\hat{X}_{z_{k}}-I\!\!E_{\theta}(\hat{X}_{z_{k}})
,
1\leq k\leq 2
, their centered versions. First of all, observe that by using Items (i), (ii) and (iii) of Lemma
B.2
, we can rewrite B as

B=I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}Y_{z_{4}}1_{\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{3}\}\cup\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{4}\}\cup\{z_{2}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{3}\}\cup\{z_{2}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{4}\}}\right].

In the remaining of the proof, we adopt the following notation. For
i,j,k,\ell\in\{1,\ldots,4\}
, we write
\{ijk\ell\}
,
\{ijk|\ell\}
,
\{ij|k\ell\}
and
\{ij|k|\ell\}
to denote, respectively, the events
\{z_{i}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{j}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{k}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{\ell}\},

\{z_{i}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{j}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{k}\not\leftrightsquigarrow z_{\ell}\}
,
\{z_{i}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{j}\not\leftrightsquigarrow z_{k}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{\ell}\}
and
\{z_{k}\not\leftrightsquigarrow z_{i}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{j}\not\leftrightsquigarrow z_{\ell}\}.

With this notation, observe that we can write

1_{\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{3}\}\cup\{z_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{4}\}\cup\{z_{2}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{3}\}\cup\{z_{2}\leftrightsquigarrow z_{4}\}}=1_{\{1234\}}+1_{\{123|4\}}+1_{\{124|3\}}+1_{\{134|2\}}+1_{\{234|1\}}\\
+1_{\{13|24\}}+1_{\{14|23\}}+1_{\{13|2|4\}}+1_{\{14|2|3\}}+1_{\{23|1|4\}}+1_{\{24|1|4\}}.

Let us denote

M=\max\{I\!\!P_{\theta}(1234),I\!\!P_{\theta}(13|24),I\!\!P_{\theta}(14|23),\\
I\!\!P_{\theta}(\{134|2\}\cap\{\tau^{R}_{z_{2}}<t_{3}\vee t_{4}\}),I\!\!P_{\theta}(\{234|1\}\cap\{\tau^{R}_{z_{1}}<t_{3}\vee t_{4}\})\}.

Step 1

Since the random variables
Y_{z_{i}}
’s and
\hat{Y}_{z_{i}}
’s are bounded by
1
almost surely, we clearly have

\left|I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}Y_{z_{4}}\left(1_{\{1234\}}+1_{\{13|24\}}+1_{\{14|23\}}\right)\right]\right|\leq 6M.

Step 2

Let
E
denote one of the following events
\{123|4\}
or
\{124|3\}
. Then, we prove the following inequality

\left|I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}Y_{z_{4}}1_{E}\right]\right|\leq 2M.

(54)

To do so, let us assume that
E=\{123|4\}
. The other case is treated similarly. By Item (vi) of Lemma
B.2
, we have that
Y_{z_{4}}=\tilde{Y}_{z_{4}}
on
E
, so that

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}Y_{z_{4}}1_{E}\right]=I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}\tilde{Y}_{z_{4}}1_{E}\right].

By using that
1_{\{123|4\}}+1_{\{1234\}}=1_{\{123\}}
is
\sigma(I^{z_{1}},I^{z_{2}},I^{z_{3}})
-measurable and the fact that
\tilde{Y}_{z_{4}}
is a centered random variable which is independent of
\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}1_{\{123\}}
(thanks to Items (ii) and (v) of Lemma
B.2
), it follows that

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}\tilde{Y}_{z_{4}}1_{E}\right]=-I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}\tilde{Y}_{z_{4}}1_{\{1234\}}\right].

Finally, we conclude like in Step 1.

Step 3

Let
E
denote one of the following events
\{134|2\}
or
\{234|1\}
. Then, we prove the following inequality

\left|I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}Y_{z_{4}}1_{E}\right]\right|\leq 5M.

(55)

To do so, let us assume that
E=\{134|2\}
. The other case is treated similarly. In the following, let us denote
E_{1}=E\cap\{\tau^{R}_{z_{2}}\geq t_{3}\vee t_{4}\}
and
E_{2}=E\cap\{\tau^{R}_{z_{2}}<t_{3}\vee t_{4}\}
. On the one hand, we use the fact that both

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}Y_{z_{4}}1_{E_{2}}\right]\text{ and }I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\right)\tilde{Y}_{z_{2}}Y_{z_{3}}Y_{z_{4}}1_{E_{2}}\right]

are upper bounded by
2I\!\!P_{\theta}\left(E_{2}\right)
. On the other hand, we know by Items (iii) and (vi) of Lemma
B.2
, that
\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}=Y_{z_{2}}=\tilde{Y}_{z_{2}}
on
E_{1}
. Combining those two properties, we can get

\left|I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}Y_{z_{4}}1_{E}\right]\right|\leq\left|I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\right)\tilde{Y}_{z_{2}}Y_{z_{3}}Y_{z_{4}}1_{E}\right]\right|+4I\!\!P_{\theta}\left(E_{2}\right).

By using that
1_{\{134|2\}}+1_{\{1234\}}=1_{\{134\}}
is
\sigma(I^{z_{1}},I^{z_{3}},I^{z_{4}})
-measurable and the fact that
\tilde{Y}_{2}
is a centered random variable which is independent of
\left(Y_{z_{1}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}Y_{z_{4}}1_{\{134\}}
(thanks to Item (v) of Lemma
B.2
), it follows that

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\right)\tilde{Y}_{z_{2}}Y_{z_{3}}Y_{z_{4}}1_{\{134|2\}}\right]=-I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\right)\tilde{Y}_{z_{2}}Y_{z_{3}}Y_{z_{4}}1_{\{1234\}}\right].

Finally, we conclude like in Step 1.

Step 4

Let
E
denote one of the following events:
\{13|2|4\}
,
\{14|2|3\}
,
\{23|1|4\}
or
\{24|1|3\}
. Then, we prove the following inequality

\left|I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}Y_{z_{4}}1_{E}\right]\right|\leq 7M.

To do so, let us assume that
E=\{13|2|4\}
. The other cases are treated similarly.
First, we combine the fact that
Y_{z_{4}}=\tilde{Y}_{z_{4}}

on
E
(this holds by Item (vi) of Lemma
B.2
) together with the decomposition
1_{\{13|2|4\}}=1_{13|2}-1_{13|24}-1_{134|2}
to deduce that

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}Y_{z_{4}}1_{\{13|2|4\}}\right]=I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}\tilde{Y}_{z_{4}}1_{\{13|2\}}\right]\\
-I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}\tilde{Y}_{z_{4}}1_{\{13|24\}}\right]-I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}\tilde{Y}_{z_{4}}1_{\{134|2\}}\right].

Now, since
\tilde{Y}_{z_{4}}
is centered and independent of
Y_{z_{1}},\hat{Y}_{z_{1}},Y_{z_{2}},\hat{Y}_{z_{2}},Y_{z_{3}}
and
1_{13|2}
(because this random variable is
\sigma(I^{z_{1}},I^{z_{2}},I^{z_{3}})
-measurable), we have that

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}\tilde{Y}_{z_{4}}1_{\{13|2\}}\right]=0,

which implies that

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}Y_{z_{4}}1_{\{13|2|4\}}\right]=-I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}\tilde{Y}_{z_{4}}1_{\{13|24\}}\right]\\
-I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}}-\hat{Y}_{z_{1}}\hat{Y}_{z_{2}}\right)Y_{z_{3}}\tilde{Y}_{z_{4}}1_{\{134|2\}}\right].

The first term on the right-hand side of the above equality is in absolute value at most
2M
. The second one can be dealt with proceeding similarly as Step 3.

Combining Steps 1 to 4, we get
|B|\leq 48M
, and Items (iii) and (iv) of Proposition
3.5
give the upper-bound
M\leq K(1-\lambda)^{t_{1}\vee t_{2}-t_{3}\wedge t_{4}}N^{-2},
which allows to conclude.
∎

By symmetry, we only need to consider the case
t_{1}\leq t_{2}
and
t_{3}\leq t_{4}.
The case
t_{1}\geq t_{3}
or
t_{2}\leq t_{3}
has already been treated in Item 6. So suppose that either
t_{1}\leq t_{3}\leq t_{2}\leq t_{4}
or that
t_{3}\leq t_{1}\leq t_{4}\leq t_{2}.
Since
I\!\!E_{\theta}[Y_{z_{i}}]=0
, we can write

B=\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{3}},Y_{z_{2}}Y_{z_{4}}\right]+\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{1}},Y_{z_{3}}\right]\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{2}},Y_{z_{4}}\right]\\
-\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{1}},Y_{z_{2}}\right]\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{3}},Y_{z_{4}}\right].

(56)

By Lemma
3.7
, it follows that

|\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{1}},Y_{z_{3}}\right]\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{2}},Y_{z_{4}}\right]|+|\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{1}},Y_{z_{2}}\right]\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{3}},Y_{z_{4}}\right]|\\
\leq KN^{-2}\left[(1-\lambda)^{|t_{1}-t_{3}|+|t_{2}-t_{4}|}+(1-\lambda)^{|t_{1}-t_{2}|+|t_{3}-t_{4}|}\right].

(57)

Also, from Item 6 above, we have that

\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{3}},Y_{z_{2}}Y_{z_{4}}\right]\leq K(1-\lambda)^{\max t_{i}-\min t_{i}}N^{-2}.

(58)

Combing (
56
), (
57
) and (
58
), we obtain the assertion.
∎

E
Temporal convergences

E.1
Proof of Equation (
32
)

This one is simple. Since we start from the stationary distribution, we have

I\!\!E_{\theta}[\widehat{m}]=T^{-1}N^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{i=1}^{N}I\!\!E_{\theta}[X_{i,t}]=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}m^{N}_{i}=\overline{m^{N}}.

Hence, the left-hand side of Equation (
32
) is equal to
\var_{\theta}(\widehat{m})
.

Then, note that
\widehat{m}=(TN)^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i,t}
so that

\displaystyle\var_{\theta}(\widehat{m})

\displaystyle\leq(TN)^{-2}\sum_{t_{1},t_{2}=1}^{T}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}=1}^{N}|\cov_{\theta}(X_{i_{1},t_{1}},X_{i_{2},t_{2}})|

\displaystyle\leq(TN)^{-1}\left[\frac{2K}{\lambda}+K(1-\lambda)+1\right],

where the last inequality comes from Lemma
3.7
.

E.2
Proof of Equation (
34
)

The proof relies on a decomposition of the error
|\widehat{w}-w^{N}_{\infty}|
(see Inequality (
59
)). Each term of the decomposition is then controlled by one of the three Propositions
E.1
,
E.7
or
E.10
. Their proofs need intermediate results, which are provided as lemmas. They are linked with martingale properties of the process (Lemmas
E.2
,
E.3
and
E.5
) or with the vanishing covariances (Lemma
E.8
).

Recall that

\widehat{w}=2\mathcal{W}_{2\Delta}-\mathcal{W}_{\Delta},\ \text{with}\ \mathcal{W}_{\Delta}=\frac{N}{T}\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor T/\Delta\rfloor}\left(\overline{Z}_{k\Delta}-\overline{Z}_{(k-1)\Delta}-\Delta\hat{m}\right)^{2}.

As it appears in the analysis below,
w^{N}_{\infty}
is the limit of
\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}
when
T,\Delta\to\infty
. Hence, the choice
\widehat{w}=\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}
would give a consistent estimator. However, the two estimators
\mathcal{W}_{2\Delta}
and
\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}
share a common bias which can be eliminated by considering
\widehat{w}=2\mathcal{W}_{2\Delta}-\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}
, which in turn drastically improves the rate of convergence. This common bias is related with the quantity
S^{N}_{\theta,t}
defined in Lemma
E.2
.

Note that by the triangle inequality, the following inequality holds:

\Big|\widehat{w}-w^{N}_{\infty}\Big|\leq 2D^{N,1}_{2\Delta,T}+D^{N,1}_{\Delta,T}+2D^{N,2}_{2\Delta,T}+D^{N,2}_{\Delta,T}+D^{N,3}_{T},

(59)

where

D^{N,1}_{\Delta,T}=\frac{N}{T}\left|\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor T/\Delta\rfloor}\left(\overline{Z}_{s\Delta}-\overline{Z}_{(s-1)\Delta}-\Delta\widehat{m}\right)^{2}\right.\\
\left.-\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor T/\Delta\rfloor}\left(\overline{Z}_{s\Delta}-\overline{Z}_{(s-1)\Delta}-I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\overline{Z}_{s\Delta}-\overline{Z}_{(s-1)\Delta}\right]\right)^{2}\right|,

(60)

D^{N,2}_{T}=\frac{N}{T}\left|\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor T/\Delta\rfloor}\left(\overline{Z}_{s\Delta}-\overline{Z}_{(s-1)\Delta}-I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\overline{Z}_{s\Delta}-\overline{Z}_{(s-1)\Delta}\right]\right)^{2}\right.\\
\left.-I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor T/\Delta\rfloor}\left(\overline{Z}_{s\Delta}-\overline{Z}_{(s-1)\Delta}-I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\overline{Z}_{s\Delta}-\overline{Z}_{(s-1)\Delta}\right]\right)^{2}\right]\right|,

(61)

and

D^{N,3}_{T}=\left|\frac{2N}{T}I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor T/2\Delta\rfloor}\left(\overline{Z}_{2s\Delta}-\overline{Z}_{2(s-1)\Delta}-I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\overline{Z}_{2s\Delta}-\overline{Z}_{2(s-1)\Delta}\right]\right)^{2}\right]\right.\\
\left.-\frac{N}{T}I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor T/\Delta\rfloor}\left(\overline{Z}_{s\Delta}-\overline{Z}_{(s-1)\Delta}-I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\overline{Z}_{s\Delta}-\overline{Z}_{(s-1)\Delta}\right]\right)^{2}\right]-w^{N}_{\infty}\right|.

(62)

In the rest of this section, propositions provide upper-bounds for the terms involved in the right-hand side of (
59
), while lemmas provide intermediate results.

Proposition E.1
.

There exists a constant
K
depending only on
\lambda
such that for all
N\geq 1
,
T\geq 2
and
1\leq\Delta\leq\lfloor T/2\rfloor
, we have that

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[D^{N,1}_{2\Delta,T}+D^{N,1}_{\Delta,T}\right]\leq K\frac{\Delta}{T}.

Using that
\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor T/\Delta\rfloor}\left(\overline{Z}_{s\Delta}-\overline{Z}_{(s-1)\Delta}\right)=\lfloor T/\Delta\rfloor\Delta\widehat{m}
and
I\!\!E_{\theta}[\overline{Z}_{\Delta}]=\Delta I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\widehat{m}\right]
, one can check that

D^{N,1}_{\Delta,T}=\frac{N}{T}\Delta^{2}\left\lfloor\frac{T}{\Delta}\right\rfloor\Big|\widehat{m}-I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\widehat{m}\right]\Big|^{2}\leq N\Delta\Big|\widehat{m}-I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\widehat{m}\right]\Big|^{2},

which implies that
I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[D^{N,1}_{\Delta,T}\right]\leq N\Delta\var_{\theta}(\widehat{m})
.
Replacing
\Delta
by
2\Delta
in the previous identity, we obtain
I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[D^{N,1}_{2\Delta,T}\right]\leq 2N\Delta\var_{\theta}(\widehat{m})
.
Hence, the result follows from Equation (
32
).
∎

To deal with the other two terms, we need to obtain a fine estimate on
\var_{\theta}\left(\overline{Z}_{\Delta}\right)=I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(\overline{U}_{\Delta}\right)^{2}\right]
(recall (
23
)). This can be done as follows. Recall that for all
t\geq 1
and
1\leq i\leq N
,

M_{i,t}=\sum_{s=1}^{t}(X_{i,s}-p_{\theta,i}(X_{s-1})),\text{ and }M_{t}=(M_{1,t},\ldots,M_{N,t}).

Let us denote
\mathcal{F}_{t}=\sigma(X_{0},\ldots,X_{t})
. From the fact that the process
(X_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is Markovian, it follows that
(M_{t})_{t\geq 1}
is a martingale with respect to the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
.

Recall that
Q^{N}=(I_{N}-(1-\lambda)A^{N})^{-1}.

Then (
24
) implies

U_{t}=Q^{N}M_{t}+(Q^{N}-I_{N})(X_{0}-X_{t}),\ t\geq 1.

(63)

Lemma E.2
.

There exists a constant
K
depending only on
\lambda
such that for all
t\geq 1
and
N\geq 1
,

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(\overline{U}_{t}\right)^{2}\right]=I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(\overline{Q^{N}M_{t}}\right)^{2}\right]+S^{N}_{\theta,t}+r^{N}_{\theta,t},

where
|r^{N}_{\theta,t}|\leq K(1-\lambda)^{t}N^{-1}
and
S^{N}_{\theta,t}
has the following properties:
|S^{N}_{\theta,t}|\leq KN^{-1}
and
|S^{N}_{\theta,2t}-S^{N}_{\theta,t}|\leq K(1-\lambda)^{t}N^{-1}
.

Starting from Equation (
63
), one can check that for any
t\geq 1
,

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(\overline{U}_{t}\right)^{2}\right]=I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(\overline{Q^{N}M_{t}}\right)^{2}\right]+H^{N}_{\theta,t},

where

H^{N}_{\theta,t}=2I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\overline{Q^{N}M_{t}}\ \overline{(Q^{N}-I_{N})(X_{0}-X_{t})}\right]+I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(\overline{(Q^{N}-I_{N})(X_{0}-X_{t})}\right)^{2}\right].

Hence, to conclude the proof, it remains to show that we can write

H^{N}_{\theta,t}=S^{N}_{\theta,t}+r^{N}_{\theta,t}
where

|r^{N}_{\theta,t}|\leq K(1-\lambda)^{t}N^{-1}
and
S^{N}_{\theta,t}
is such that
|S^{N}_{\theta,2t}-S^{N}_{\theta,t}|\leq K(1-\lambda)^{t}N^{-1}
, for all
t\geq 1
and
N\geq 1
. To see that, first of all, observe that

\displaystyle\overline{(Q^{N}-I_{N})(X_{0}-X_{t})}

\displaystyle=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(Q^{N}(i,j)-I_{N}(i,j))(X_{j,0}-X_{j,t})

\displaystyle=N^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(X_{j,0}-X_{j,t})(c^{N}_{j}-1),

where
c^{N}=(Q^{N})^{\intercal}1_{N}
, so that

\displaystyle\left(\overline{(Q^{N}-I_{N})(X_{0}-X_{t})}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=N^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(c^{N}_{i}-1)(c^{N}_{j}-1)(X_{i,0}-X_{i,t})(X_{j,0}-X_{j,t})

\displaystyle=N^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(c^{N}_{i}-1)(c^{N}_{j}-1)(Y_{i,0}-Y_{i,t})(Y_{j,0}-Y_{j,t}),

where the second equality follows from the definition of
Y_{i,t}=X_{i,t}-I\!\!E_{\theta}[X_{i,t}]
and the stationarity of the process
(X_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
. Using the stationarity once more and the above equation, we then deduce that

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(\overline{(Q^{N}-I_{N})(X_{0}-X_{t})}\right)^{2}\right]=2N^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(c^{N}_{i}-1)(c^{N}_{j}-1)\left[\cov_{\theta}(Y_{i,0},Y_{j,0})\right.\\
\left.-\cov_{\theta}(Y_{i,0},Y_{j,t})\right],

so that by applying Lemma
3.7
and using Inequality (
84
), we obtain that

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(\overline{(Q^{N}-I_{N})(X_{0}-X_{t})}\right)^{2}\right]=\frac{2}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(c^{N}_{i}-1)(c^{N}_{j}-1)\cov_{\theta}(Y_{i,0},Y_{j,0})+r^{N}_{\theta,t},

where
|r^{N}_{\theta,t}|\leq K(1-\lambda)^{t}N^{-1}
.

Now, proceeding similarly as above, one can check that

\displaystyle I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\overline{Q^{N}M_{t}}\ \overline{(Q^{N}-I_{N})(X_{0}-X_{t})}\right]=N^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}c^{N}_{i}(c^{N}_{j}-1)I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[M_{i,t}(X_{j,0}-X_{j,t})\right].

For each
1\leq i,j\leq N
and
t\geq 1
, denote

S^{N}_{\theta,t}(i,j)=I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[M_{i,t}(X_{j,0}-X_{j,t})\right].

We claim that there exists a constant
K>0
such that for all
1\leq i,j\leq N
and
t\geq 1
, the following inequality holds:
|S^{N}_{\theta,t}(i,j)|\leq K(\delta_{ij}+N^{-1})
and

|S^{N}_{\theta,2t}(i,j)-S^{N}_{\theta,t}(i,j)|\leq K(1-\lambda)^{t}N^{-1}.

Once this claim is proved, using Inequality (
84
) and Lemma
3.7
it is immediate to check that
H^{N}_{\theta,t}=S^{N}_{\theta,t}+r^{N}_{\theta,t}
, where

S^{N}_{\theta,t}=N^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left[c^{N}_{i}(c^{N}_{j}-1)S^{N}_{\theta,t}(i,j)+2(c^{N}_{i}-1)(c^{N}_{j}-1)\cov_{\theta}(X_{i,0},Y_{j,0})\right]

satisfies
|S^{N}_{\theta,2t}-S^{N}_{\theta,t}|\leq K(1-\lambda)^{t}N^{-1}
and
|S^{N}_{\theta,t}|\leq KN^{-1}
, implying the result.

To prove the claim above, we first write

S^{N}_{\theta,t}(i,j)=I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[M_{i,t}(X_{j,0}-X_{j,t})\right]=\sum_{s=1}^{t}I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[(X_{i,s}-p_{\theta,i}(X_{s-1}))(X_{j,0}-X_{j,t})\right]

and then use the fact that

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[(X_{i,s}-p_{\theta,i}(X_{s-1}))X_{j,0}\right]=I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[X_{j,0}I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[(X_{i,s}-p_{\theta,i}(X_{s-1}))|\mathcal{F}_{s-1}\right]\right]=0

to deduce that

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[M_{i,t}(X_{j,0}-X_{j,t})\right]=\sum_{s=1}^{t}\left(I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[p_{\theta,i}(X_{s-1})X_{j,t}\right]-I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[X_{i,s}X_{j,t}\right]\right).

Next, we use (
1
) and the stationarity to obtain that

\sum_{s=1}^{t}I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[p_{\theta,i}(X_{s-1})X_{j,t}\right]=tm^{N}_{j}(\mu-(1-\lambda)L^{N,\bullet-}_{i})\\
+(1-\lambda)\sum_{k=1}^{N}A^{N}(i,k)\sum_{s=1}^{t}I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[X_{k,s-1}X_{j,t}\right].

Hence, using that
I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[X_{k,s-1}X_{j,t}\right]=\cov_{\theta}(X_{k,s-1},X_{j,t})+m_{k}^{N}m_{j}^{N}
and the stationarity once more, one can check that

\sum_{s=1}^{t}I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[p_{\theta,i}(X_{s-1})X_{j,t}\right]=tm^{N}_{j}(\mu-(1-\lambda)L^{N,\bullet-}_{i}+(1-\lambda)(A^{N}m^{N})_{i})\\
+(1-\lambda)\sum_{k=1}^{N}A^{N}(i,k)\sum_{s=1}^{t}\cov_{\theta}\left(X_{k,0},X_{j,s}\right),

and similarly that

\sum_{s=1}^{t}I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[X_{i,s}X_{j,t}\right]=tm_{i}^{N}m^{N}_{j}-\sum_{s=0}^{t-1}\cov_{\theta}\left[X_{i,0},X_{j,s}\right].

Therefore, combining the last two equations and using (
14
), we obtain that

S^{N}_{\theta,t}(i,j)=(1-\lambda)\sum_{k=1}^{N}A^{N}(i,k)\sum_{s=1}^{t}\cov_{\theta}\left(X_{k,0},X_{j,s}\right)+\sum_{s=0}^{t-1}\cov_{\theta}\left(X_{i,0},X_{j,s}\right),

so that Lemma
3.7
allows us to conclude that

\left|S^{N}_{\theta,2t}(i,j)-S^{N}_{\theta,t}(i,j)\right|\leq\\
\leq(1-\lambda)\sum_{k=1}^{N}|A^{N}(i,k)|\sum_{s=t+1}^{2t}|\cov_{\theta}\left(X_{k,0},X_{j,s}\right)|+\sum_{s=t}^{2t-1}|\cov_{\theta}\left(X_{i,0},X_{j,s}\right)|\\
\leq\frac{K(1-\lambda)^{t}}{N}\left(\sum_{s=0}^{t-1}(1-\lambda)^{s}\right)\left[(1-\lambda)^{2}N|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|A^{N}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{\infty}+1\right]\leq\frac{K(1-\lambda)^{t}}{N}.

Finally, using once more Lemma
3.7
, one can easily check that
|S^{N}_{\theta,t}(i,j)|\leq K(\delta_{ij}+N^{-1})
proving the claim.
∎

The next result will be important to find the leading term of
I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(\overline{Q^{N}M_{t}}\right)^{2}\right]
.

Lemma E.3
.

For all
t\geq 1,
the following equality holds.

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[(X_{t}-p_{\theta}(X_{t-1}))^{2}\right]=m^{N}-(m^{N})^{2}+r^{N}_{\theta},

where
\|r^{N}_{\theta}\|_{\infty}\leq KN^{-1}
for some constant
K>0
depending only on
\lambda
.

Remark E.4
.

Note that
X_{t}
,
p_{\theta}(X_{t-1})
and
m^{N}
are vectors so that the square terms in Lemma
E.3
have to be interpreted in terms of the Hadamard product of vectors. Also, the remainder term
r^{N}_{\theta}
does not depend on time
t
because the process
(X_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is assumed to be stationary.

First, observe that

\displaystyle I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[(X_{i,t}-p_{i,\theta}(X_{t-1}))^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[X_{i,t}\right]-I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[p^{2}_{i,\theta}(X_{t-1})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m^{N}_{i}-I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[p^{2}_{i,\theta}(X_{0})\right],

where the second equality follows by the stationarity. Now, from (
1
), we have that

p_{i,\theta}(X_{0})=\left(\mu-(1-\lambda)L^{N,\bullet-}_{i}\right)+(1-\lambda)(A^{N}X_{0})_{i},

so that

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[p^{2}_{i,\theta}(X_{0})\right]=(\mu-(1-\lambda)L^{N,\bullet-}_{i})^{2}+2(\mu-(1-\lambda)L^{N,\bullet-}_{i})(1-\lambda)(A^{N}I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[X_{0}\right])_{i}\\
+(1-\lambda)^{2}I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[((A^{N}X_{0})_{i})^{2}\right].

(64)

Next, notice that

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[((A^{N}_{\theta}X_{0})_{i})^{2}\right]=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}A^{N}(i,j)A^{N}(i,k)I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[X_{k,0}X_{j,0}\right],

so that using that
I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[X_{k,0}X_{j,0}\right]=m^{N}_{j}m^{N}_{k}+\cov_{\theta}(X_{k,0},X_{j,0})
, we deduce that

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[((A^{N}X_{0})_{i})^{2}\right]=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}A^{N}(i,j)m^{N}_{j}\right)^{2}+\widetilde{r}^{N}_{\theta,i}

(65)

where

\widetilde{r}^{N}_{\theta,i}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}A^{N}(i,j)A^{N}(i,k)\cov_{\theta}(X_{k,0},X_{j,0}).

Using the fact that
|A^{N}(i,j)|\leq 1/N
and Lemma
3.7
, we have

|\widetilde{r}^{N}_{\theta,i}|\leq N^{-2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}|\cov_{\theta}[X_{k,0},X_{j,0}]|\leq N^{-2}(N+N^{2}K(1-\lambda)N^{-1})\leq KN^{-1}.

Finally, recalling that
m^{N}=I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[X_{0}\right]
, it follows from (
64
) and (
65
) that

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[p^{2}_{i,\theta}(X_{0})\right]=\left[(\mu-(1-\lambda)L^{N,\bullet-}_{i})+(1-\lambda)(A^{N}m^{N})_{i}\right]^{2}+(1-\lambda)^{2}\widetilde{r}^{N}_{\theta,i}.

The result then follows from equation (
15
).
∎

As a consequence of Lemma
E.3
, we will now find the leading term of
I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(\overline{Q^{N}M_{t}}\right)^{2}\right]
.

Lemma E.5
.

For any
t\geq 1
and
N\geq 1
,

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\Big|\overline{Q^{N}M_{t}}\Big|^{2}\right]=\frac{t}{N}w^{N}_{\infty}+r^{N}_{\theta,t},

where
w^{N}_{\infty}
is defined in (
31
) and
r^{N}_{\theta,t}
is such that
|r^{N}_{\theta,t}|\leq KtN^{-2}
for some
K
depending only on
\lambda
.

We start observing that

\overline{Q^{N}M_{t}}=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}Q^{N}(i,j)M_{j,t}=N^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{N}c^{N}_{j}M_{j,t},

and then we use that for all
s\neq h
and
1\leq j,k\leq N
,

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[(X_{i,s}-p_{i,\theta}(X_{s-1}))(X_{k,h}-p_{k,\theta}(X_{h-1}))\right]=0,

to deduce that

\displaystyle I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\Big|\overline{Q^{N}M_{t}}\Big|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N^{-2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}c^{N}_{j}c^{N}_{k}I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[M_{j,t}M_{k,t}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N^{-2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(c^{N}_{j})^{2}\sum_{s=1}^{t}I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(X_{j,s}-p_{j,\theta}(X_{s-1})\right)^{2}\right].

Hence, it follows from Lemma
E.3
that

\displaystyle I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\Big|\overline{Q^{N}M_{t}}\Big|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N^{-2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(c^{N}_{j})^{2}\sum_{s=1}^{t}I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(X_{j,s}-p_{j,\theta}(X_{s-1})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle tN^{-2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(c^{N}_{j})^{2}m^{N}_{j}(1-m^{N}_{j})+tN^{-2}\langle(c^{N})^{2},r^{N}_{\theta}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle tN^{-1}w^{N}_{\infty}+tN^{-2}\langle(c^{N})^{2},r^{N}_{\theta}\rangle.

Finally, by applying Hölder inequality and then using that
\|v\|_{1}\leq N\|v\|_{\infty}
for
v\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, we obtain

\langle(c^{N})^{2},r^{N}_{\theta}\rangle\leq\|(c^{N})^{2}\|_{\infty}\|r^{N}_{\theta}\|_{1}\leq\|(c^{N})^{2}\|_{\infty}N\|r^{N}_{\theta}\|_{\infty},

and the result follows from inequality (
84
) and Lemma
E.3
.
∎

For later use, let us mention the following immediate corollary of Lemmas
E.2
and
E.5
.

Lemma E.6
.

There exists a constant
K>0
depending only on
\lambda
such that for all
t\geq 1
and
N\geq 1
,

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[\left(\overline{U}_{t}\right)^{2}\right]\leq KtN^{-1}.

First, observe that
w^{N}_{\infty}
defined in (
31
) satisfies
|w^{N}_{\infty}|\leq\lambda^{-2}/4
by
Inequality (
84
), and then combine this fact with Lemmas
E.2
and
E.5
.
∎

We are now in position to deal with the term
D^{N,3}_{T}
defined in (
62
).

Proposition E.7
.

There exists a constant
K>0
depending only on
\lambda
such that for all
N\geq 1
,
T\geq 2
and
1\leq\Delta\leq\lfloor T/2\rfloor
,

I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[D^{N,3}_{T}\right]\leq K\left(\frac{1}{N}+\frac{(1-\lambda)^{\Delta}}{\Delta}+\frac{\Delta}{T}\right).

First, observe that by stationarity,

D^{N,3}_{T}=\Big|\frac{2N}{T}\lfloor T/{2\Delta}\rfloor I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[(\overline{U}_{2\Delta})^{2}\right]-\frac{N}{T}\lfloor T/{\Delta}\rfloor I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[(\overline{U}_{\Delta})^{2}\right]-w^{N}_{\infty}\Big|.

Then, by using the triangle inequality, one can check that

\Big|\frac{2N}{T}\lfloor T/{2\Delta}\rfloor I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[(\overline{U}_{2\Delta})^{2}\right]-\frac{N}{T}\lfloor T/{\Delta}\rfloor I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[(\overline{U}_{\Delta})^{2}\right]-w^{N}_{\infty}\Big|\\
\leq\Big|\frac{N}{\Delta}\left(I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[(\overline{U}_{2\Delta})^{2}\right]-I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[(\overline{U}_{\Delta})^{2}\right]\right)-w^{N}_{\infty}\Big|+\frac{2N}{T}\left(I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[(\overline{U}_{2\Delta})^{2}\right]+I\!\!E_{\theta}\left[(\overline{U}_{\Delta})^{2}\right]\right).

Then Lemmas
E.2
,
E.5
and
E.6
imply the result.
∎

It remains to deal with
D^{N,2}_{\Delta,T}
and
D^{N,2}_{2\Delta,T}
. To that end, we shall use two additional lemmas. The first one is the following.

Lemma E.8
.

There exists a constant
K>0
depending only on
\lambda
such that for all
N\geq 1
,
\Delta\geq 1
and
t\geq 2,

\cov_{\theta}\left[\left(\overline{U}_{\Delta}\right)^{2},\left(\overline{U}_{t\Delta}-\overline{U}_{(t-1)\Delta}\right)^{2}\right]\leq KN^{-2}(1-\lambda)^{(t-2)\Delta}.

First, use that
\overline{U}_{t}=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{s=1}^{t}Y_{{i,s}}
to deduce that

\cov_{\theta}\left[\left(\overline{U}_{\Delta}\right)^{2},\left(\overline{U}_{t\Delta}-\overline{U}_{(t-1)\Delta}\right)^{2}\right]=N^{-4}\sum_{z_{1}\in F_{1}}\sum_{z_{2}\in F_{1}}\sum_{z_{3}\in F_{t}}\sum_{z_{4}\in F_{t}}\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}},Y_{z_{3}}Y_{z_{4}}\right],

where
z_{k}=(i_{k},t_{k})
for
k\in\{1,\ldots,4\}
, and
F_{s}:=\{1,\ldots,N\}\times\{(s-1)\Delta+1,\ldots,s\Delta\}
for
s\geq 1
. In the rest of the proof, we shall denote
{\bf z}=(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4})
and
B({\bf z})=\cov_{\theta}\left[Y_{z_{1}}Y_{z_{2}},Y_{z_{3}}Y_{z_{4}}\right].

Notice that the covariance function
B({\bf z})
is symmetric with respect to
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, as well as with respect to
z_{3}
and
z_{4}
. For each
p\in\{1,\ldots,6\}
and
s\geq 1
, we denote
\mathcal{C}_{p,s}
the set of all vectors
{\bf z}\in F_{1}\times F_{1}\times F_{s}\times F_{s}
satisfying the conditions of Item
p
of Lemma
3.8
and such that
t_{1}\leq t_{2}
and
t_{3}\leq t_{4}
.
With this notation, one can check using the symmetry of the covariance function that the following inequality holds:

\cov_{\theta}\left[\left(\overline{U}_{\Delta}\right)^{2},\left(\overline{U}_{t\Delta}-\overline{U}_{(t-1)\Delta}\right)^{2}\right]\leq 4N^{-4}\sum_{p=1}^{6}\sum_{{\bf z}\in\mathcal{C}_{p,t}}|B({\bf z})|.

(66)

Notice that
\mathcal{C}_{1,t}=\mathcal{C}_{2,t}=\mathcal{C}_{5,t}=\emptyset
for
t\geq 2
, so that we need only to control the sum of covariances for the cases
p\in\{3,4,6\}
. This is done in the 3 steps below.

Step 1.
Here, we show that there exists a constant
K>0
such that for all
N\geq 1
,
\Delta\geq 1
and
t\geq 2
,

\sum_{{\bf z}\in\mathcal{C}_{3,t}}|B({\bf z})|\leq KN(1-\lambda)^{(t-2)\Delta+1}.

First, note that Item 3 of Lemma
3.8
implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf z}\in\mathcal{C}_{3,t}}|B({\bf z})|

\displaystyle\leq KN^{2}\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{\Delta}\sum_{t_{3}=(t-1)\Delta+1}^{t\Delta}(1-\lambda)^{(t_{3}-t_{1})}N^{-1}

\displaystyle=KN(1-\lambda)^{(t-2)\Delta+1}\sum_{k=0}^{\Delta-1}(1-\lambda)^{k}\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{\Delta}(1-\lambda)^{\Delta-t_{1}}.

Step 1 follows by noticing that
\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{\Delta}(1-\lambda)^{\Delta-t_{1}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\Delta-1}(1-\lambda)^{k},
and then by using that
\sum_{k=0}^{\Delta-1}(1-\lambda)^{k}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(1-\lambda)^{k}\leq\lambda^{-1}
.

Step 2.
Here, we prove that there exists a constant
K>0
such that for all
N\geq 1
,
\Delta\geq 1
and
t\geq 2
,

\sum_{{\bf z}\in\mathcal{C}_{4,t}}|B({\bf z})|\leq KN(1-\lambda)^{(t-2)\Delta+1}.

First, by Remark
3.9
, we can write

\sum_{{\bf z}\in\mathcal{C}_{4,t}}|B({\bf z})|=\sum_{{\bf z}\in\mathcal{C}_{4,t}}{{\mathbf{1}}}_{\{z_{1}=z_{2},z_{3}\neq z_{4}\}}|B({\bf z})|+\sum_{{\bf z}\in\mathcal{C}_{4,t}}{{\mathbf{1}}}_{\{z_{1}\neq z_{2},z_{3}=z_{4}\}}|B({\bf z})|.

To conclude, we apply Item 4 of Lemma
3.8
to obtain an upper bound for each one of the terms on the right hand side of the above identity. Since both terms are treated very similarly, we explain how we handle the first term only. By applying Item 4 of Lemma
3.8
to the first one, we obtain that

\sum_{{\bf z}\in\mathcal{C}_{4,t}}{{\mathbf{1}}}_{\{z_{1}=z_{2},z_{3}\neq z_{4}\}}|B({\bf z})|\leq KN^{3}\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{\Delta}\sum_{t_{3}=(t-1)\Delta+1}^{t\Delta}\sum_{t_{4}=t_{3}}^{t\Delta}(1-\lambda)^{(t_{4}-t_{3})+(t_{3}-t_{1})}N^{-2}.

Now, since for any
(t-1)\Delta+1\leq t_{3}\leq t\Delta
,

\sum_{t_{4}=t_{3}}^{t\Delta}(1-\lambda)^{(t_{4}-t_{3})}=\sum_{k=0}^{t\Delta-t_{3}}(1-\lambda)^{k}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\Delta-1}(1-\lambda)^{k}\leq\lambda^{-1},

(67)

we can then proceed as in Step 1 to conclude that

\sum_{{\bf z}\in\mathcal{C}_{4,t}}{{\mathbf{1}}}_{\{z_{1}=z_{2},z_{3}\neq z_{4}\}}|B({\bf z})|\leq KN(1-\lambda)^{(t-2)\Delta+1}.

Step 3.
Here, we prove that there exists a constant
K>0
such that for all
N\geq 1
,
\Delta\geq 1
and
t\geq 2
,

\sum_{{\bf z}\in\mathcal{C}_{6,t}}|B({\bf z})|\leq KN^{2}(1-\lambda)^{(t-2)\Delta+1}.

By applying Item 6 of Lemma
3.8
and inequality (
67
), one can check that

\sum_{{\bf z}\in\mathcal{C}_{6,t}}|B({\bf z})|\leq KN^{4}\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{\Delta}\sum_{t_{2}=t_{1}}^{\Delta}\sum_{t_{3}=(t-1)\Delta+1}^{t\Delta}N^{-2}(1-\lambda)^{t_{3}-t_{1}}.

Some algebraic computations imply that
